To address this limitation, we propose SAM3D-Phys, a framework that integrates scene reconstruction with generative 3D priors of SAM3D to recover physically simulatable objects. Our approach first reconstructs the scene from multi-view images to obtain scene geometry and partial observations of objects. We then leverage SAM3D to infer complete object geometry from these partial observations. To ensure that the recovered objects remain consistent with the reconstructed scene, we restore scene-consistent object states through two complementary strategies: a physics-constrained spatial optimization algorithm that iteratively aligns the recovered object to its original location, and a mask-guided appearance distillation module that refines texture fidelity based on the observed images. By recovering complete object geometry and restoring its pose and appearance within the scene, SAM3D-Phys produces clean object representations suitable for physics-based simulation, enabling simultaneous and physically consistent interactive simulation of multiple objects within a reconstructed scene. Project page: https://chnxindong.github.io/sam3d-phys/

</details>


### [28] [AdaState: Self-Evolving Anchors for Streaming Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30349)
*Yusuf Dalva,Pinar Yanardag*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: 核心思想：不要把首帧当作固定世界锚点，而是在每一步维护一个随内容共同演化的隐藏状态。这样可打破首帧锁定效应，在不额外增加外部模块的前提下提升视频的运动性与时序推进。 / Core idea: stop using the first frame as a frozen world anchor; instead, maintain a hidden state that evolves with the generated content at every step. This breaks first-frame locking and improves motion and temporal progression without adding an external module.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 现有自回归视频扩散模型过度依赖首帧在KV cache中的特权位置。由于首帧最干净、误差最少，它会吸引过多注意力，导致模型压制运动与视角变化，生成时间上较浅、过于静态的视频。 / Existing autoregressive video diffusion models are overly anchored to the first frame because its privileged, clean KV-cache representation attracts disproportionate attention, suppressing motion and viewpoint changes and producing temporally shallow, overly static videos.

Method: 方法是在每个生成chunk中与视频内容一起去噪一个从不被渲染的隐藏latent state。模型在每一步通过关注前一状态和当前内容来生成新的场景锚点；同时采用相对时间建模，使每一步具有相同的位置结构，状态转移在各chunk间共享，从而在无需外部模块的情况下把递归机制引入生成过程。 / The method denoises an unrendered hidden latent state alongside visible content at every chunk. At each step, the model attends to the previous state and current content to form a new scene anchor, while using a relative-time formulation with identical positional structure and shared transition dynamics across chunks, introducing recurrence without external modules.

Result: 实验表明，自适应状态显著提升了视频动态表现，带来更丰富的运动和更自然的场景演化。 / Experiments show that the adaptive state substantially improves video dynamics, enabling richer motion and more natural scene evolution.

Conclusion: 使用可自适应演化的隐藏状态替代对首帧的静态依赖，可以显著改善自回归视频扩散模型的视频动态性，使运动、镜头变化和场景推进更自然。 / Replacing the static first-frame anchor with an adaptive evolving hidden state significantly improves dynamics in autoregressive video diffusion, yielding more natural motion, camera movement, and scene progression.

Abstract: Autoregressive video diffusion models generate streaming video by producing frames sequentially, conditioning each chunk on previously generated content. These models are structurally anchored to the first frame: its key-value representation occupies a privileged position in the attention cache and serves as the primary scene reference throughout generation. As the cleanest and most error-free position in the cache, this anchor draws disproportionate attention, suppressing video dynamics, and locking scene composition to the initial viewpoint even as the scene naturally evolves. The result is a temporally shallow video in which motion, camera movement, and scene progression are dampened in favor of static consistency. To address this, we replace the static anchor with an adaptive state, a hidden latent that the model denoises alongside content at every chunk but never renders. Rather than referencing a frozen first frame, the model generates its own scene anchor at each step by attending to both the previous state and the current content, producing a reference that evolves with the generated content. Unlike standard video generation, which encodes an absolute notion of time, our formulation treats time as relative: every generation step sees the same positional structure regardless of how far generation has progressed, and the state transition is identical at every chunk. Together, these properties introduce a recurrence into the generation process, where denoising serves as the transition function, and the KV cache serves as the carrier, requiring no external module. Experiments demonstrate that the adaptive state substantially improves video dynamics, enabling richer motion and natural scene progression within generated videos.

</details>


<div id='cs.CL'></div>

# cs.CL [[Back]](#toc)

### [29] [From Context Shift to Stylistic Collapse: Why Training Objectives Matter More Than Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28826)
*Rohan Mahapatra*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Instruction tuning, not just generation style, systematically distorts language distributions in modern LLMs. RLHF does not fix this, but sufficiently strong control can substantially restore diversity and reduce collapse even in much smaller models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work mainly compared AI-generated text with human text, but this paper aims to examine how the LLM training and alignment pipeline itself distorts linguistic distributions, creating structural changes that standard quality metrics may miss.

Method: The paper evaluates 17 language models ranging from 410M to over 100B parameters using 24 linguistically motivated probes, comparing base, instruction-tuned, and RLHF-aligned variants, and testing different intervention strengths (lambda=1.0 and lambda=5.0) to measure changes in entropy, punctuation use, diversity, and repetition.

Result: Instruction-tuned models show extreme amplification of discourse and structural feature collapse and strong suppression of complex punctuation. RLHF does not significantly worsen these divergences relative to instruction tuning alone. Weak intervention increases collapse, whereas strong control improves linguistic behavior, surpasses frontier models despite much smaller scale, raises distinct-4 and vocabulary diversity, and lowers repetition.

Conclusion: Current alignment pipelines, especially instruction tuning, substantially reshape linguistic feature distributions by collapsing entropy and suppressing complex punctuation; stronger control can mitigate this effect more effectively than weak regularization, while RLHF and model scale do not meaningfully solve the problem.

Abstract: In modern LLMs, linguistic features function not as stylistic artifacts but as probes of probability mass, allocated under training alignment objectives. Language models trained with contemporary pipelines exhibit severe reshaping of linguistic features, leading to extreme language re-distribution. While previous stylometric analyses explored linguistic differences between AI-generated and human texts, we focus on the reshaping plaguing the LLM training pipeline itself. We analyze 17 models (410M-100B+ parameters) across 24 linguistically-motivated probes, documenting that instruction-tuned systems systematically collapse language entropy along discourse and structural dimensions (mean amplification: 1,949-16,853%, peaks: 5,181-209,675%), while selectively suppressing complex punctuation to 3.2-23.2% of baseline frequencies. These effects do not worsen under RLHF, as divergence patterns are statistically indistinguishable (p > 0.25) across matched base and instruction-tuned model pairs. Weak intervention (lambda=1.0) exacerbates collapse by 240%, while strong control (lambda=5.0) achieves 40.5% improvement and outperforms frontier models by 96.7-98.2% despite 200-1000x scale disadvantage. Additionally, lambda=5.0 delivers 15% higher distinct-4, 27% higher vocabulary diversity, and 78% lower repetition than moderate regularization, establishing that alignment requires sufficient control strength, not merely distributional smoothing. Our findings underscore how modern LLMs reallocate stylistic probability mass, despite RLHF and scale. More broadly, our work reveals a structural limitation of current alignment pipelines: preference optimization reshapes language distributions invisible to standard quality metrics yet detectable through distributional probes, with implications for AI detection, training data contamination, and long-term linguistic evolution.

</details>


### [30] [RightNow-Arabic-0.5B-Turbo: An Open Sub-1B Arabic Language Model via Vocabulary Injection and Edge-First Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28827)
*Jaber Jaber,Osama Jaber*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A fully open 0.5B Arabic-specialized LLM was built by adapting Qwen2.5-0.5B with Arabic tokenizer expansion, continued pretraining, instruction tuning, DPO, and checkpoint merging; it delivers best-in-class small-model Arabic performance and is lightweight enough for edge deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open Arabic LLMs are polarized between very small multilingual models that under-serve Arabic and much larger Arabic-specialized models that are too heavy for local or edge use; there was no truly open, compact, Arabic-specialized model with released weights.

Method: Starting from Qwen2.5-0.5B, the authors expanded the tokenizer with 27,032 Arabic tokens using mean-subtoken initialization, continued Arabic pretraining on 504M tokens with FSDP, FlashAttention variable-length packing, and Liger fused kernels, then performed supervised fine-tuning on 129,116 Arabic instruction pairs, direct preference optimization on 6,750 preference pairs, and finally weight-soup merged three checkpoints.

Result: The merged 518M-parameter model achieved 35.9% mean accuracy on COPA-ar, Arabic HellaSwag, and ArabicMMLU, matched Falcon-H1-1.5B on COPA-ar at one-third the size, recovered 67% of SILMA-9B’s mean score with 1/18 the parameters, and an edge quantized build ran at 398 MB and 635 tokens/s on a single H100 via llama.cpp.

Conclusion: RightNow-Arabic-0.5B-Turbo shows that a compact 0.5B Arabic-specialized LLM can outperform other open models in its size class and approach much larger Arabic models while remaining practical for edge deployment.

Abstract: Open Arabic large language models split into two classes: sub-1B multilingual models that treat Arabic as an afterthought (Qwen2.5-0.5B, Falcon-H1-0.5B), and 7B-70B Arabic-specialized models that require a server to run (Jais, AceGPT, ALLaM, SILMA). The one published attempt at a sub-2B Arabic-specialized model, Kuwain-1.5B, never released its weights. We present RightNow-Arabic-0.5B-Turbo, a 518M-parameter Arabic-specialized decoder LLM built on Qwen2.5-0.5B. The pipeline adds 27,032 Arabic tokens via mean-subtoken initialization, continues pretraining on 504M Arabic tokens on 8xH100 with FSDP, FlashAttention varlen packing, and Liger fused kernels, then applies supervised fine-tuning on 129,116 Arabic instruction pairs with response-only loss masking, direct preference optimization on 6,750 Arabic preference pairs, and weight soup merging across three checkpoints. On three lm-evaluation-harness Arabic benchmarks (COPA-ar, Arabic HellaSwag, ArabicMMLU) the merged model reaches 35.9% mean accuracy, beats every same-class open model, ties Falcon-H1-1.5B on COPA-ar (58.4%) at one-third the size, and recovers 67% of SILMA-9B's mean at 1/18 the parameters. The edge build quantizes to 398 MB (q4_k_m) and delivers 635 tokens/s at batch size 1 on a single H100 via llama.cpp. All code (5,555 lines across 25 scripts), weights (bf16, int8, and four GGUF quantizations), and benchmark scripts are released at https://huggingface.co/RightNowAI/RightNow-Arabic-0.5B-Turbo.

</details>


### [31] [SERC: LDPC-Inspired Semantic Error Correction for Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28837)
*Gyumin Kim,Juhwan Park,Jaeha Kim,Seunggyun Han,Kyungrak Son,Ikbeom Jang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces SERC, an LDPC-inspired sparse verification framework that treats LLM generation like error correction over a noisy semantic channel. By checking only a small set of strategically generated verification queries against external evidence, it reduces hallucinations more accurately and cheaply than prior self-correction and RAG baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing self-correction methods for LLM hallucinations are limited by self-bias because models often cannot reliably detect their own errors without external grounding. The work is motivated by the need for a more reliable and efficient hallucination mitigation approach with lower verification overhead.

Method: The paper models text generation as a semantic noisy channel and treats generated responses as noise-corrupted codewords. Inspired by LDPC codes, it proposes SERC, which generates low-density verification queries, checks them against external evidence, and uses the results to detect and correct factual errors efficiently instead of performing dense fact verification.

Result: On LongForm Bio and TruthfulQA using Llama-3-8B and Qwen2.5-14B, SERC outperforms intrinsic self-correction methods and strong retrieval-augmented baselines, with especially strong gains in factual precision measured by FactScore. It also allows smaller language models to exceed larger baselines in hallucination reduction and information preservation.

Conclusion: SERC is a training-free, model-agnostic semantic error correction framework for retrieval-augmented generation that reduces hallucinations more effectively than intrinsic self-correction and strong RAG baselines, while lowering verification cost through sparse checking.

Abstract: While Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities, their reliability is significantly compromised by hallucinations. Existing intrinsic self-correction methods attempt to address this, but often fail due to self-bias, where models struggle to identify errors in their own outputs without external verification. To overcome these limitations, we propose the LDPC-inspired semantic error correction for retrieval-augmented generation (SERC), providing a theoretical framework to interpret and mitigate LLM hallucinations. We reformulate the text generation process as a semantic noisy channel, treating generated responses as noise-corrupted codewords. Inspired by low-density parity-check (LDPC) codes, SERC employs a sparse verification strategy: instead of exhaustively checking all facts, it generates low-density verification queries and validates them against external evidence to efficiently detect and correct errors. We evaluate SERC on LongForm Bio and TruthfulQA benchmarks using Llama-3-8B and Qwen2.5-14B. Experimental results demonstrate that SERC outperforms both intrinsic self-correction methods and strong retrieval-augmented baselines, demonstrating significant gains especially in factual precision (FactScore). Notably, SERC enables small language models (SLMs) to surpass the performance of larger baselines in hallucination reduction and information preservation. Our findings demonstrate that SERC provides a training-free, model-agnostic solution that significantly reduces verification overhead compared to dense methods, achieving an optimal trade-off between cost and fidelity in resource-constrained environments.

</details>


### [32] [Specialty-Specific Medical Language Model for Immune-Mediated Diseases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28838)
*Veysel Kocaman,Gursev Pirge,Yigit Gul,Ace Vo,Zhenya Nargizyan,David Talby*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper creates an expert-annotated clinical NER dataset and shows that a domain-specific transformer with clinical embeddings extracts immunology/infectious disease entities much better than generic, zero-shot, or prompted LLM approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: General-purpose NLP systems struggle to capture detailed and inconsistent disease terminology in immunology and infectious disease narratives, limiting structured information extraction from free-text clinical documents.

Method: The study built a manually annotated dataset of 371 clinical case reports with 12 entity classes, then compared several NER strategies: MedicalNER with healthcare-specific embeddings, a BERT-based token classification model, and zero-shot NER systems. Models were evaluated using F1 score under a shared protocol.

Result: The best model was a transformer-based NER system trained with clinical-domain embeddings, achieving an F1 score of 0.89 and consistently surpassing baseline and zero-shot methods. The prompted LLM baseline performed substantially worse, especially on span-consistent fine-grained entity boundary detection.

Conclusion: A domain-specific transformer-based NER model with clinical-domain embeddings can accurately extract fine-grained immunology and infectious disease entities from free-text case reports, outperforming baseline, zero-shot, and prompted LLM approaches.

Abstract: Extracting detailed clinical information from free-text medical narratives remains a practical challenge for researchers and healthcare systems. Terminology for immune-mediated and infectious diseases is especially inconsistent across sources, which often limits the ability of general-purpose Natural Language Processing (NLP) systems to capture the relevant biomedical concepts with sufficient granularity. We developed a domain-specific Named Entity Recognition (NER) model tailored to identify disease-related entities occurring in immunology and infectious disease contexts. We assembled and manually annotated a dataset of 371 case reports in collaboration with two clinical specialists, defining twelve entity classes covering immune-mediated and infectious conditions as well as related symptoms and clinical descriptors. We evaluated several modeling strategies, including the MedicalNER architecture with multiple healthcare-specific embeddings, a BERT-based token classification model, and zero-shot NER systems. The strongest performance was obtained with a transformer-based model trained on clinical-domain embeddings, which reached an F1 score of 0.89, consistently outperforming baseline and zero-shot approaches. The combination of specialized embeddings and expert annotation proved particularly valuable for capturing nuanced disease terminology and improving generalization across heterogeneous biomedical text. The prompted LLM baseline achieved substantially lower performance under the same evaluation protocol, reflecting difficulties in producing span-consistent outputs for fine-grained entity boundaries despite detailed prompting. The resulting model provides a structured way to analyze case reports and can support downstream tasks such as cohort identification, disease monitoring, and clinical decision support.

</details>


### [33] [How Consistent Are LLM Agents? Measuring Behavioral Reproducibility in Multi-Step Tool-Calling Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28840)
*Abel Yagubyan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper studies whether tool-using LLM agents act the same way twice on the same input, and proposes an empirical framework to measure consistency in tool selection, order, and arguments in realistic structured tool-calling settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although LLM agents with tool-calling are increasingly used in production, it is unclear whether the same agent behaves consistently across repeated identical invocations, especially in structured tool-calling settings with typed parameters and side effects.

Method: The paper conducts a systematic empirical study of behavioral consistency in multi-step tool-calling agents by repeatedly invoking the same agent on identical inputs and comparing tool choice, execution order, and arguments across runs.

Result: From the abstract alone, the key result is the introduction of a consistency evaluation framework for structured multi-step tool-calling agents; specific quantitative findings are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: Current production LLM tool-calling agents have an under-studied reliability issue: repeated identical inputs may not lead to the same multi-step behavior, so behavioral consistency must be measured explicitly.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents with tool-calling capabilities are increasingly deployed in production systems, yet a fundamental reliability question remains under-explored: does the same agent behave the same way twice? We present a systematic empirical study of behavioral consistency in multi-step tool-calling agents, measuring whether agents select the same tools, in the same order, with the same arguments, across repeated identical invocations. Unlike prior work on consistency in ReAct-style agents(search-only, free-text actions), we study the richer setting of structured tool-calling interfaces with typed parameters and consequential side effects.

</details>


### [34] [Thoughts-as-Planning: Latent World Models for Chain-of-Thoughts Optimization via Reinforcement Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28842)
*Dong Liu,Yanxuan Yu,Ying Nian Wu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Treat reasoning-chain tuning as a planning problem in latent space: learn a world model of chain edits, plan edits with optimization/RL, and achieve better performance plus interpretability than prior baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing reasoning-chain tuning methods depend on black-box heuristics or gradient-free search, limiting interpretability, generalization, and sample efficiency; the paper aims to provide a principled, structured optimization framework.

Method: It proposes Thoughts-as-Planning, which models an LLM as a partially observable environment, learns a latent world model of reasoning-chain edits and their downstream effects, builds a proximity-preserving embedding space for reasoning chain-response dynamics, and performs planning via gradient descent or reinforcement learning across token-, segment-, and instruction-level edits.

Result: Experiments on language understanding and generation tasks show the method outperforms state-of-the-art reasoning-chain tuning baselines in efficiency, robustness, and generalization, while also improving interpretability through structured planning trajectories.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that framing reasoning-chain optimization as planning in a latent semantic space yields more efficient, robust, generalizable, and interpretable tuning than prior heuristic or gradient-free methods.

Abstract: The success of large language models (LLMs) across diverse NLP tasks has elevated the importance of reasoning chain optimization as a critical step in aligning model behavior with task objectives. Existing reasoning chain tuning methods often rely on black-box heuristics or gradient-free search, which lack interpretability, generalization, and sample efficiency. In this work, we introduce \textbf{Thoughts-as-Planning}, a novel framework that formalizes reasoning chain optimization as a sequential decision-making process over a latent semantic space. We model the LLM as a partially observable environment and learn a latent world model that simulates the effect of reasoning chain edits on downstream outputs. A proximity-preserving embedding space is constructed to encode reasoning chain-response dynamics, enabling planning via gradient descent or reinforcement learning. Our method supports multi-scale abstraction, allowing reasoning chain edits at token, segment, and instruction levels to be integrated into a unified planner. Through extensive experiments on language understanding and generation tasks, we demonstrate that Thoughts-as-Planning outperforms state-of-the-art reasoning chain tuning baselines in efficiency, robustness, and generalization, while offering interpretability through its structured planning trajectory. Our code is available at https://github.com/FastLM/Thoughts-as-Planning.

</details>


### [35] [Large language models reorganize representational geometry during in-context learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28854)
*Hua-Dong Xiong,Li Ji-An,Robert C. Wilson,Kwonjoon Lee,Xue-Xin Wei*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: 这篇论文说明：LLM做上下文学习分类时，不只是‘会不会算法’的问题，还取决于内部表征空间是否容易被在线整理成更可分的几何结构；模型的行为近似于一边形成原型、一边重塑表征来完成分类。/ The paper argues that ICL classification in LLMs is not only about having the right algorithm, but also about whether internal representations can be reorganized online into a more separable geometry; the behavior resembles building prototypes while reshaping representations to classify.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 虽然已有工作证明LLM在ICL中可以实现特定算法，但尚不清楚高维表征空间的几何如何影响ICL。作者受神经科学中“分类即表征解缠结”观点启发，提出ICL可能依赖于对任务相关表征的在线解缠结。/ Prior work showed that LLMs can implement algorithms in ICL, but it remains unclear how the geometry of high-dimensional representation space affects ICL. Inspired by the neuroscience view that classification is representation untangling, the authors hypothesize that ICL depends on online untangling of task-relevant representations.

Method: 作者构造了一类分类任务，其中标签由模型自身内部、且结构已知的表征定义；随后分析不同表征结构下的ICL分类表现，并跟踪上下文处理过程中表征几何是否发生重组与可分性提升，同时用原型式算法刻画模型行为。/ The authors build classification tasks whose labels are defined by the model’s own internal representations with known structure, then measure ICL performance across representational structures, track geometric reorganization and separability changes during context processing, and model behavior with a prototype-like algorithm.

Result: ICL性能与底层分类任务的表征结构系统相关；成功的ICL伴随几何重组，使在线可分性提高；LLM行为可由一种在整合证据同时重塑表征以支持分类的原型式算法较好解释。/ ICL performance systematically correlates with the representational structure of the underlying task; successful ICL is accompanied by geometric reorganization that increases online separability; and LLM behavior is well described by a prototype-like algorithm that integrates evidence while reshaping representations for classification.

Conclusion: ICL的效果受表征几何结构约束；当任务相关表征能在上下文中被在线“解缠结”并变得更可分时，LLM的ICL分类表现更好。/ ICL is constrained by representational geometry; LLMs perform better when task-relevant representations can be untangled online and made more separable during in-context processing.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) exhibit remarkable flexibility: they can adapt to novel tasks from in-context examples without any parameter updates, a capability known as in-context learning (ICL). Prior work on synthetic tasks has shown that ICL can implement specific algorithms, demonstrating architectural competence, and mechanistic analyses have identified key circuits that support this behavior. However, because in-context computation -- regardless of its algorithmic form -- relies on transformations in high-dimensional representation space, it remains unclear how the geometry of that space shapes ICL effectiveness. Motivated by the neuroscience view of classification as the untangling of neural representations, we hypothesize that ICL depends on the successful online untangling of task-relevant representations. To test this idea, we study how LLMs classify in-context examples whose labels are defined by the model's own internal representations with known structure. We show that ICL performance correlates systematically with the representational structure of the underlying classification task and that successful ICL is accompanied by geometric reorganization that increases online separability. We further find that LLM behavior is well described by a prototype-like algorithm that integrates evidence while reshaping representations to support classification. These findings offer a geometric account of ICL in pretrained LLMs, establish representational geometry as a mechanistic constraint on ICL, and quantify the gap between what pretrained representations afford and what in-context learning can exploit.

</details>


### [36] [From Data to Insights: Exploring Program-of-Thoughts Prompting for Chart Summarization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28874)
*Yutong Qu,Wei Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Use zero-shot Program-of-Thought with a new chart-to-dictionary representation so lightweight VLMs can compute chart statistics via Python and summarize charts as well as prior methods, with better efficiency and factual grounding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing chart summarization methods face two main problems: chart understanding requires both semantic visual interpretation and numerical reasoning, and recent visual language model approaches are often computationally expensive and lack reliable mechanisms to verify statistical facts. The work is motivated by the need for a lighter and more factually grounded solution.

Method: The paper uses zero-shot learning to prompt lightweight visual language models to reason through Python programs as intermediate steps. It introduces a chart-to-dictionary auxiliary task instead of the more rigid chart-to-table representation, and integrates this representation with a Program-of-Thought reasoning framework to compute valid summary statistics for chart understanding.

Result: Experiments show that the proposed strategy achieves performance comparable to existing chart summarization methods on both semantic metrics and factual metrics.

Conclusion: A zero-shot Program-of-Thought strategy combined with a chart-to-dictionary auxiliary task enables lightweight visual language models to perform chart summarization with competitive semantic quality and factual correctness while remaining more efficient than heavier approaches.

Abstract: Charts play a critical role in conveying numerical data insights through structured visual representations. However, semantic visual understanding and numerical reasoning requirements hinder the accurate description of charts, interpreting a challenging task in chart summarization. Despite recent advancements in visual language models (VLMs), approaches lack robust mechanisms for verifying statistical fact correctness and are computationally heavy. To address this gap, this paper explores a strategy of using zero-shot learning to motivate the lightweight VLMs to perform computational reasoning, via Python programs as intermediaries to derive valid summary statistics for chart understanding. Specifically, we introduce a novel chart-to-dictionary auxiliary task, offering a more flexible representation compared to traditional chart-to-table methods, making it particularly well-suited for integration with the Program-of-Thought (PoT) strategy. Experimental results demonstrate our strategy performs on par with existing chart summarization methods across semantic and factual metrics. Code is available on https://anonymous.4open.science/r/ZeroShot-PoT-C2T-5A6B.

</details>


### [37] [GrowLoop: Self-Evolving Conversation Evaluation Seeded by Human](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28882)
*Yihang Lin,Yunze Gao,Zeyang Lin,Dongbo Li,Kun Peng,Chenglong Song,Yue Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: GrowLoop is a self-evolving benchmark system for evaluating conversational human-likeness that learns and updates rubrics and cases from minimal human seeds, better matches human judgments, and stays useful as models and scenarios change.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluation methods for human-likeness in open-ended conversation fail to jointly address three core challenges: tacit and hard-to-explicitly-formalize criteria, variability and legitimate disagreement in human judgments, and the evolving nature of what counts as human-like as models and expectations change.

Method: The paper proposes GrowLoop, a self-evolving conversation evaluation system. Starting from minimal human seed annotations, LLM agents iteratively extract and refine evaluation rubrics via Heuristic Learning. It enforces human-AI agreement where annotators converge, requires only plausibility where annotators diverge, and uses a Rubric-Case co-evolution mechanism to continuously update both evaluation criteria and cases, with new seeds added when the target shifts.

Result: When applied to human-likeness evaluation, the generated rubrics substantially outperform existing methods in alignment with human judgments, reveal overlooked issues, effectively distinguish models across capability tiers, identify model weaknesses, generalize to new scenarios, and adapt as models improve.

Conclusion: GrowLoop offers a comprehensive self-evolving evaluation paradigm for human-likeness in open-ended conversation, enabling continuous adaptation to changing model capabilities and human expectations while improving alignment with human judgment.

Abstract: With the rapid advancement of large language models, evaluating human-likeness in open-ended conversation has become increasingly important. However, human-likeness is a form of tacit knowledge that humans perceive intuitively, yet the underlying criteria resist explicit formulation. Human judgments vary widely, with strong agreement on some cases and legitimate disagreement on others. Meanwhile, the criteria behind human judgments remain implicit, leaving no clear basis for constructing cases. Further, what counts as human-like is not static, but evolving with model capability and human expectations. Despite progress in evaluation methods such as expert-authored benchmarks, Reward Models, and self-evolving benchmarks, none addresses all three challenges simultaneously. Therefore, we propose GrowLoop, a self-evolving conversation evaluation system that continuously adapts as models advance and scenarios shift. With minimal human seed annotations as the first mover, LLM agents iteratively extract and refine evaluation rubrics through Heuristic Learning. Human-AI agreement is required where annotators converge, while only plausibility is expected where they diverge. Moreover, the Rubric-Case co-evolution mechanism enables continuous evolution, expanded through new seeds when the evaluation target moves. Applied to human-likeness evaluation in open-ended conversation, the generated rubrics not only substantially outperform existing methods in alignment with human judgments, but also uncover issues that annotators overlook. The resulting benchmark effectively discriminates models across capability tiers and reveals where they fall short, while generalizing to new scenarios and adapting as models advance. Our work shifts the benchmarking paradigm from manual updates or difficulty scaling to comprehensive, continuous self-evolution.

</details>


### [38] [Error as a Lens: Probing LLM Reasoning through Synthetic Misconception Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29007)
*Xinming Yang,Jun Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A two-agent LLM system generates synthetic student mistakes aligned with specific misconception types, helping build labeled error datasets without real student data; targeted mistake generation is hard, and answer-grounding helps most.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is that personalized tutoring, teacher training, and education research need targeted synthetic misconceptions, but privacy and IRB constraints make labeled corpora of real student errors difficult to obtain. While LLMs can easily produce generic wrong answers, generating errors that reflect a specified cognitive failure mode is much more challenging and more useful.

Method: The method uses a Generation Agent (GA) to produce an incorrect solution conditioned on a target misconception class, and an Examination Agent (EA) to verify that the solution is both actually wrong and consistent with the intended class. The framework is evaluated on questions from the TheoremQA dataset using a five-class taxonomy adapted from the revised Bloom's taxonomy.

Result: The results show that the framework can generate reusable, class-targeted synthetic error data across the five misconception classes on TheoremQA. The study also shows that targeted error generation is substantially more difficult than free-form incorrect-answer generation, and that answer-grounding contributes more than expanded examples or external textbook content.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that a two-agent framework can generate synthetic student errors targeted to a five-class misconception taxonomy, providing a practical recipe for building class-stratified error datasets when real labeled student data is scarce. It also finds that targeted error generation is meaningfully harder than unconstrained wrong-answer generation, and that grounding on the answer matters more than adding more examples or textbook context.

Abstract: Personalized tutoring, teacher training, and education research need access to \emph{targeted} synthetic misconceptions, but privacy and IRB constraints make labelled corpora of real student errors scarce. LLMs could in principle generate synthetic errors at scale, but producing an arbitrary wrong answer is easy for a modern LLM while producing one that matches a specified cognitive failure mode is much harder. We present a framework that generates errors targeted to a five-class taxonomy adapted from the revised Bloom's taxonomy, evaluated on questions from the TheoremQA dataset. A Generation Agent (GA) drafts a candidate erroneous solution conditioned on a target class, and an Examination Agent (EA) judges whether the draft is incorrect and class-consistent. The framework yields a reusable recipe for building class-stratified synthetic error datasets where authentic student corpora are unavailable. As a secondary diagnostic, targeted error generation is substantially harder than free-form incorrect-answer generation, and answer-grounding contributes more than expanded examples or external textbook content.

</details>


### [39] [OmniRetrieval: Unified Retrieval across Heterogeneous Knowledge Sources](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29250)
*Jinheon Baek,Soyeong Jeong,Sangwoo Park,Woongyeong Yeo,Minki Kang,Patara Trirat,Heejun Lee,Sung Ju Hwang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: OmniRetrieval unifies access to heterogeneous knowledge sources by routing natural-language queries into source-specific queries, improving retrieval performance without losing the structural advantages of each source type.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world information is distributed across structurally diverse sources such as unstructured text, relational databases, and knowledge/property graphs, but existing retrieval systems are fragmented because they typically support only one source and one query language at a time.

Method: The paper proposes OmniRetrieval, a framework that maps natural-language queries to appropriate knowledge sources, generates source-native queries for each selected source, and executes them on the corresponding native engines across text, relational tables, and graph-based systems.

Result: On a benchmark covering 13 datasets and 309 knowledge bases, OmniRetrieval outperforms single-source baselines across text, relational, and graph-structured sources.

Conclusion: OmniRetrieval provides a general-purpose retrieval interface across heterogeneous knowledge sources by preserving each source's native structure and query semantics instead of forcing homogenization.

Abstract: Real-world information needs require access to structurally diverse knowledge sources, from unstructured text and relational tables to knowledge graphs and property graphs. Existing retrievers, however, operate over one source at a time under a fixed query language, leaving the broader landscape of available knowledge fragmented behind incompatible interfaces. A natural attempt at unification would collapse these sources into a shared space, but this erases the structural affordances (such as schemas, ontologies, compositional operators) that give each source its expressive power. Effective retrieval over diverse knowledge, therefore, requires not homogenization but an overarching layer that meets each source on its own terms. To achieve this, we present OmniRetrieval, a framework that takes any natural-language query, identifies appropriate knowledge sources, and dispatches source-native queries to their native execution engines. Across an extensive benchmark spanning 13 datasets and 309 distinct knowledge bases over text, relational, and graph-structured sources, OmniRetrieval exceeds single-source baselines, demonstrating that it can serve as a general-purpose interface to the heterogeneous sources while preserving the structural distinctions that make each source valuable.

</details>


### [40] [DynSess: Dynamic Session-Level Evaluation and Optimization Framework for Role-Playing Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29256)
*Rongsheng Zhang,Jiji Tang,Junnan Ren,Zuyi Bao,Weijie Chen,Ruofan Hu,Zhou Zhao,Tangjie Lv,Yan Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A session-level framework for role-playing LLMs that evaluates whole dialogues and trains on session rewards, improving long-horizon character consistency and human preference efficiently.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing role-playing LLM methods mostly evaluate and optimize at the turn level, which misses long-horizon behaviors such as sustained character identity and interaction quality across an entire conversation.

Method: The paper proposes DynSess, a unified session-level framework consisting of DynSess-Eval for rubric-based full-session scoring, multi-turn lookahead search to build high-quality trajectories, and DynSess-Character training with two variants: DSPO (off-policy) and GSRPO (on-policy).

Result: Experiments indicate DynSess-Eval aligns much better with human judgments than prior evaluators, and DynSess-Character matches the strongest character model in blind human evaluation despite using substantially fewer parameters, while preserving role consistency and interaction quality.

Conclusion: DynSess shows that session-level evaluation and optimization significantly improve role-playing LLMs over long multi-turn conversations, producing better alignment with human judgment and strong character consistency with fewer parameters.

Abstract: Role-playing with large language models is fundamentally a session-level task, requiring agents to sustain character identity and interaction quality across extended multi-turn conversations. Yet existing evaluation and optimization methods remain largely turn-level, failing to capture long-horizon quality. We propose DynSess, a unified session-level framework for role-playing agents. DynSess-Eval scores complete dialogue sessions via rubrics targeting long-horizon behaviors. Leveraging its session-level rewards, we construct high-quality training trajectories through multi-turn lookahead search and train DynSess-Character with two complementary variants: DSPO (off-policy) and GSRPO (on-policy). Experiments show that DynSess-Eval aligns with human judgments substantially better than prior evaluators, and blind human evaluation further shows that DynSess-Character matches the strongest character model despite using substantially fewer parameters, while maintaining strong role consistency and interactive ability. Our dataset and code will be released to facilitate future research.

</details>


### [41] [Prompt-Level Reward Specifications for Open-Ended Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29275)
*Zijun Weng,Xiaohui Hu,Shuangyong Song,Yongxiang Li,Kaidong Yu,Xuanjing Huang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a prompt-only framework that explicitly defines reward criteria using reusable rubrics and executable constraint checkers, then mixes them with a global quality score to create a hybrid reward. This improves ranking and RL performance on open-ended tasks without needing human preferences, references, or a trained reward model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing reward methods for open-ended tasks often keep success criteria implicit or only handle narrowly verifiable cases, even though tasks like instruction following, writing, and decision support require handling local requirements, holistic preferences, and explicit constraints. The paper is motivated by the need to make these criteria explicit, reusable, and trainable without human preference labels or reference answers.

Method: The method is a prompt-level reward specification framework that separates reward specification from reward computation. From prompts alone, it builds reusable task-adaptive rubrics and executable hard-constraint checkers offline, then combines artifact-anchored rubric scores and code-based verification scores with an independent global quality score into a normalized hybrid reward.

Result: Experiments show that the proposed reward improves offline reward-model-style response ranking and supports online reinforcement learning on multiple open-ended benchmarks. Ablation studies indicate that rubrics, global scoring, and executable verification each provide complementary supervision.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that making prompt-specific reward criteria explicit through reusable rubrics and executable constraint checkers yields better supervision for open-ended post-training than relying only on implicit scalar rewards. The hybrid reward improves both response ranking and online RL across several open-ended benchmarks.

Abstract: Open-ended post-training benefits from rewards that make prompt-specific success conditions explicit, rather than relying only on post-hoc scalar scores. In instruction following, writing, and decision-support tasks, response quality depends on local requirements, holistic preferences, and explicit constraints, but existing reward methods often leave these criteria implicit or cover only narrowly verifiable cases. We propose a prompt-level reward specification framework that separates reward specification from reward computation. Given only prompts, our framework constructs reusable task-adaptive rubrics and executable hard-constraint checkers offline, making reward criteria explicit before training and reusable across rollouts. At scoring time, artifact-anchored rubric and code scores are combined with an independent global score for residual holistic quality, yielding a normalized hybrid reward over requirement satisfaction, holistic quality, and deterministic constraints. The framework requires no human preference annotations, reference answers, or a separately trained reward model. Experiments show that the resulting reward improves offline RM-style response ranking and supports online reinforcement learning across multiple open-ended benchmarks. Ablations further show that rubrics, global scoring, and executable verification provide complementary supervision.

</details>


### [42] [Accommodation Goes Both Ways: Studying Linguistic Convergence Between Humans and Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29278)
*Terra Blevins*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LLMs mimic users' language style much more than humans mimic LLMs; humans treat LLMs linguistically about like they treat other people, but LLMs strongly overadapt to users.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs are becoming embedded in everyday communication, it is important to understand whether and how they influence human language behavior. The paper is motivated by the open question of whether humans and LLMs mutually accommodate each other's linguistic styles during dialogue, and whether this interaction differs from ordinary human-human conversation.

Method: The authors conduct a large-scale empirical study of multi-turn human-LLM conversations using WildChat, a corpus of real-world ChatGPT transcripts. They apply an asymmetric convergence metric to measure how much each side shifts toward the other's linguistic style, examining both function-word and open-class lexical features across eight languages, and compare human convergence with human-human baselines.

Result: The main result is that LLMs significantly overconverge toward users on both function-word and open-class features in all eight languages studied. In contrast, human convergence in human-LLM dialogue remains broadly aligned with established human-human baseline levels, indicating no unusual increase in accommodation toward LLMs.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that linguistic accommodation in human-LLM conversations is strongly asymmetric: LLMs adapt heavily to users' linguistic style across multiple feature types and languages, whereas humans do not adapt to LLMs more than they would to another human interlocutor.

Abstract: As LLMs become increasingly integrated into daily life, understanding how their presence will shape human linguistic behavior is an open question. We present a large-scale study of linguistic convergence in human-LLM dialogue, examining how humans and LLMs accommodate each other's linguistic style during multi-turn conversations. Using an asymmetric convergence metric on WildChat, a corpus of real-world ChatGPT transcripts, we find that while LLMs significantly overconverge toward their users on both function word and open-class features across eight languages, human convergence rates in this setting are broadly consistent with human-human baselines. These findings suggest that accommodation in human-LLM dialogue is asymmetric: while LLMs dramatically overfit to their users' style, humans linguistically accommodate LLMs no differently than they would another person.

</details>


### [43] [MusTBENCH: Benchmarking and Advancing Temporal Grounding in Music LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29300)
*Daeyong Kwon,Qiyu Wu,Shinobu Kuriya,Junghyun Koo,Shuyang Cui,Zhi Zhong,Wei-Hsiang Liao,Hiromi Wakaki,Yuki Mitsufuji*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper studies whether audio-language models can link music-related answers to the right time segments in audio. It introduces a benchmark, MusTBENCH, and a four-stage training recipe, MusT, showing that current models are weak at temporal grounding but can be substantially improved.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Large Audio-Language Models show promising music understanding performance, but it is unclear whether their answers are grounded in the correct temporal regions of audio, which is crucial because many musical cues are temporally localized.

Method: The paper introduces MusTBENCH, a music-expert-validated benchmark with five temporally grounded QA tasks, and proposes MusT, a four-stage temporal optimization pipeline including music encoder adaptation, LLM adaptation, supervised fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning-based optimization.

Result: Experiments on MusTBENCH show that existing Large Audio-Language Models perform poorly on precise temporal grounding, while the proposed MusT method significantly improves performance over strong baselines.

Conclusion: Temporal grounding is a key missing capability in current Large Audio-Language Models for music understanding, and the proposed MusTBENCH benchmark plus MusT optimization recipe provide an effective path to evaluate and improve it.

Abstract: Recent Large Audio-Language Models (LALMs) have demonstrated promising abilities in understanding musical content. However, whether their responses are grounded in the correct temporal regions of the audio remains underexplored. This limitation is particularly critical for music understanding, where key information often occurs as temporally localized events, such as instrument entries and rhythmic transitions. To address this gap, we introduce MusTBENCH, a music-expert-validated benchmark designed to evaluate temporal grounding in LALMs through five temporally grounded question-answering tasks. To further improve temporal grounding in existing models, we propose MusT, a novel four-stage temporal optimization recipe spanning music encoder adaptation, LLM adaptation, LLM supervised fine-tuning, and RL-based optimization. Experiments on MusTBENCH show that existing LALMs struggle with precise temporal grounding, while MusT brings significant improvements over strong baselines. These results establish temporal grounding as a key missing capability in current LALMs and position MusTBENCH as a challenging benchmark for future research in temporally grounded music understanding.

</details>


### [44] [GrepSeek: Training Search Agents for Direct Corpus Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29307)
*Alireza Salemi,Chang Zeng,Atharva Nijasure,Jui-Hui Chung,Razieh Rahimi,Fernando Diaz,Hamed Zamani*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: GrepSeek lets an LLM search a text corpus by running shell commands instead of only querying a retriever. It is trained with a staged pipeline plus GRPO, runs efficiently with parallelized execution, and outperforms prior methods on seven QA benchmarks, though it is weaker when wording mismatch is large.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM search agents mostly depend on retrievers built on indexed, pre-computed document representations. The authors aim to explore a complementary paradigm where the agent directly interacts with the raw corpus as an executable search environment, potentially improving task-oriented evidence finding and composition without relying solely on traditional retrieval infrastructure.

Method: The paper proposes GrepSeek, a direct corpus interaction search agent. It uses a two-stage training pipeline: (1) cold-start trajectory generation with an answer-aware Tutor and answer-blind Planner to produce verified, causally grounded search traces, and (2) reinforcement learning refinement with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). It also introduces a semantics-preserving sharded-parallel shell execution engine to scale retrieval efficiently.

Result: Across seven open-domain question answering benchmarks, GrepSeek achieves the best overall token-level F1 and Exact Match. Its sharded-parallel execution engine speeds up shell-based retrieval by up to 7.6x while preserving byte-exact equivalence to sequential shell execution. The analysis also shows lexical-only interaction struggles when query wording differs substantially from corpus surface forms.

Conclusion: GrepSeek demonstrates that direct corpus interaction via executable shell commands is a practical and competitive alternative to conventional retriever-based search for LLM agents, achieving state-of-the-art overall token-level F1 and Exact Match across seven open-domain QA benchmarks while remaining limited on queries with strong surface-form variation.

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) search agents have shown strong promise for knowledge-intensive language tasks through multiple rounds of reasoning and information retrieval. Most existing systems access information using a retriever that takes a keyword or natural language query and returns a ranked list of documents using an index of pre-computed document representations. In this work, we explore a complementary perspective in which the search agent treats the corpus itself as the search environment and finds evidence by issuing executable shell commands. We introduce GrepSeek, an optimized direct corpus interaction (DCI) search agent that trains a compact search agent to find, filter, and compose evidence from large text corpora. To address the instability of learning behavior directly with reinforcement learning on large corpora, we propose a two-stage training pipeline. First, we construct a cold-start dataset using an answer-aware Tutor and answer-blind Planner to generate verified, causally grounded search trajectories. Second, we refine the initialized policy with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), allowing the agent to improve its task-oriented search behavior through direct interaction with the corpus. To make DCI practical at scale, we further use a semantics-preserving sharded-parallel execution engine that accelerates shell-based retrieval by up to $7.6\times$ while preserving byte-exact equivalence with sequential execution of the shell command. Experiments across seven open-domain question answering benchmarks show that GrepSeek achieves the strongest overall token-level $F_1$ and Exact Match. Our analysis also highlights the limitations of purely lexical interaction on queries with substantial surface-form variation, suggesting DCI as a practical and competitive method for search agents that can complement existing retrieval paradigms in the real world.

</details>


### [45] [SURGENT: A Surgical Multi-Agent Assistance System Across the Perioperative Workflow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29368)
*Dongsheng Shi,Yue Li,Xin Yi,Yongyi Cui,Huawei Feng,Linlin Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SURGENT is a surgical multi-agent system with planning, collaboration, retrieval, and dual-memory mechanisms that delivers better perioperative decision support than standard LLM-based baselines, while enabling private local deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern surgical care requires intelligent, transparent, and auditable support across the perioperative workflow, but standard web-based LLMs are limited by context length, incomplete memory handling, and weak traceability for surgical applications.

Method: The paper proposes SURGENT, which integrates a Tree-of-Thought planner, multi-department collaboration agents, retrieval-augmented reasoning using clinical guidelines and biomedical literature, and a dual-memory design for long-term patient history plus short-term working summaries.

Result: Across five perioperative tasks, SURGENT outperforms baseline LLMs and existing medical multi-agent systems; ablation studies also show the advantage of DeepSeek as a locally deployable backbone for privacy-preserving use.

Conclusion: SURGENT is a practical and trustworthy surgical multi-agent assistance system that improves perioperative reasoning quality, aligns recommendations more closely with patient histories, and supports privacy-preserving local deployment.

Abstract: The intricate nature of modern surgical care necessitates intelligent systems that can synthesize extensive patient records, support collaborative decision-making, and provide transparent, auditable reasoning across the entire perioperative workflow. Although web-based Large Language Models (LLMs) possess advanced reasoning capabilities, they are ill-equipped for surgical applications due to critical limitations: input length constraints, incomplete memory management, and limited traceability. To address this issue, we present SURGENT, a surgical multi-agent assistance system that combines a Tree-of-Thought planner, multi-department collaboration agents, and retrieval-augmented reasoning with clinical guidelines and biomedical literature. SURGENT features a novel memory design that manages both long-term patient histories and short-term working summaries, enabling more complete, contextualized, and consistent reasoning. Experimental evaluations across five key perioperative tasks - case analysis, surgical plan simulation, safety monitoring, complication risk assessment, and rehabilitation guidance - show that SURGENT outperforms baseline LLMs and existing medical multi-agent frameworks, yielding recommendations more closely aligned with patient histories. Ablation studies further highlight the advantage of DeepSeek as a locally deployable backbone model, enabling privacy-preserving deployment without reliance on centralized services. These results position SURGENT as a practical and trustworthy advancement toward intelligent, equitable, and secure surgical assistance systems.

</details>


### [46] [Revisiting Observation Reduction for Web Agents: Comprehensive Evaluation with a Lightweight Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29397)
*Masafumi Enomoto,Ryoma Obara,Haochen Zhang,Masafumi Oyamada*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes MFS coverage as a fast proxy for evaluating HTML reduction in LLM web agents, cutting evaluation cost by over 100x while still predicting task success well; using this signal, they build a pruner that speeds agents up 2.2x to 3.1x while keeping most performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing HTML observations for LLM web agents are very long, but it is unclear which reduction methods actually improve total agent latency without hurting task success. Direct end-to-end benchmarking is too expensive and slow, making systematic comparison difficult.

Method: The paper defines the Minimal Failure Set (MFS) as the smallest set of HTML elements whose removal causes task failure, then measures a reduction method by coverage: the fraction of cases where the method preserves the full MFS. The authors validate correlation between coverage and true end-to-end success on WorkArena L1 and WebLinx, and use MFS training data to optimize a pruning program.

Result: Coverage strongly correlates with end-to-end success rate and yields over 100x cumulative evaluation speedup on both benchmarks. The study finds many extractive reduction methods are too costly or too domain-specific to reduce latency effectively. Their optimized pruning program achieves 2.2x faster per-step latency with 84% retained success on WorkArena L1, and 3.1x faster with 89% retained success on WebLinx.

Conclusion: A lightweight proxy evaluation based on Minimal Failure Set (MFS) coverage can reliably predict end-to-end web-agent performance at far lower cost, and practical HTML reduction methods only help latency when they are either computationally cheap or optimized for the domain.

Abstract: HTML observations in LLM-based web agents are extremely long, and while many reduction methods have been proposed, it remains unclear which methods reduce overall agent latency while maintaining performance. The main obstacle is the high cost of end-to-end evaluation: in our experiments, evaluating 11 methods across 32 configurations on 33 tasks of WorkArena L1 required 232.4 cumulative hours. To address this, we propose a lightweight evaluation framework based on the Minimal Failure Set (MFS), the minimal set of HTML elements whose removal causes task failure. We define coverage as the fraction of instances in which a reduction method fully retains the MFS, which serves as a proxy metric that requires neither web access nor LLM inference. We validate that coverage strongly correlates with end-to-end success rate, with over 100$\times$ speedup in cumulative evaluation time on both benchmarks. Using this framework, we find that extractive HTML reduction methods require either high computation cost or domain-specific optimization to reduce agent latency while maintaining performance. Building on this, we optimize a pruning program on MFS training data, achieving 2.2$\times$ faster per-step latency on WorkArena L1 while retaining 84\% of the original success rate, and 3.1$\times$ faster on WebLinx while retaining 89\%.

</details>


### [47] [Beyond Bilingual Transfer: Multilingual Code-Switching in Instruction Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29414)
*Shunta Asano,Jeonghun Baek,Toshihiko Yamasaki*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Mixing English, Japanese, Korean, and Chinese in instruction-tuning data improves multilingual LLM performance on Belebele, suggesting code-switching helps in multilingual, not just bilingual, transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work showed benefits of code-switching data for bilingual transfer, mainly between English and one target language, but it remained unclear whether those gains extend to true multilingual settings with three or more languages.

Method: The paper performs instruction tuning with multilingual code-switching data that mixes four languages within the same context, and evaluates resulting multilingual understanding using the Belebele benchmark.

Result: Across experiments on four languages, simple sentence-level multilingual code-switching data consistently improves average performance on multilingual understanding tasks.

Conclusion: Multilingual sentence-level code-switching instruction tuning across English, Japanese, Korean, and Chinese consistently improves average multilingual understanding, showing that code-switching is effective beyond bilingual transfer.

Abstract: Recent studies have shown that code-switching data (CSD), in which multiple languages are mixed within the same context, can improve cross-lingual transfer and multilingual alignment in large language models (LLMs). However, existing studies primarily focus on bilingual transfer between English and a target language, leaving multilingual settings involving three or more languages largely unexplored. In this work, we investigate multilingual code-switching instruction tuning across four languages: English, Japanese, Korean, and Chinese. We evaluate multilingual understanding on Belebele. Our experiments show that simple sentence-level multilingual CSD consistently improves average multilingual performance across all four languages, indicating that multilingual code-switching can be effective beyond bilingual transfer settings.

</details>


### [48] [Learning Design Skills as Memory Policies for Agentic Photonic Inverse Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29421)
*Shengchao Chen,Ting Shu,Sufen Ren*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SkillPCF turns PCF inverse design into a memory-driven closed-loop learning problem and outperforms prior methods by reusing physics-aware design skills to find better fiber designs with fewer expensive simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PCF inverse design is difficult because candidate structures must satisfy coupled optical objectives while electromagnetic simulation is expensive, and prior methods do not effectively accumulate reusable knowledge across iterative trials.

Method: The paper proposes SkillPCF, a closed-loop agent framework combining a physics-guided memory skill bank, reinforcement-learned skill selection, and simulator-grounded skill evolution for iterative PCF inverse design.

Result: Using a real-world dataset of 479 expert interaction traces, 2,507 spans, and 553 memory-dependent evaluation queries, experiments across multiple LLM backbones and classical baselines show better design-quality/efficiency trade-offs for SkillPCF.

Conclusion: SkillPCF demonstrates that treating photonic crystal fiber inverse design as a memory-policy learning problem improves design quality and search efficiency under limited simulation budgets.

Abstract: Photonic crystal fiber (PCF) inverse design remains challenging because candidate geometries must satisfy coupled optical targets under expensive electromagnetic simulation. Existing pipelines improve surrogate prediction or one-shot parameter recommendation, but they do not accumulate reusable design knowledge across iterative trials. We formulate PCF inverse design as a memory-policy learning problem and propose SkillPCF, a closed-loop agent framework that combines a physics-guided memory skill bank, reinforcement-learned skill selection, and simulator-grounded skill evolution. We further construct a real-world dataset with 479 expert interaction traces (2,507 spans) and 553 memory-dependent evaluation queries covering dispersion engineering, loss optimization, and multi-objective design. Experiments across multiple LLM backbones and classical baselines show that SkillPCF achieves stronger design-quality and efficiency trade-offs under practical simulation budgets, demonstrating the effectiveness of our proposed memory-skill learning paradigm for physics-aware PCF inverse design.

</details>


### [49] [FinGuard: Detecting Financial Regulatory Non-Compliance in LLM Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29427)
*Huaixia Dou,Jie Zhu,Minghao Wu,Shuo Jiang,Junhui Li,Lifan Guo,Feng Chen,Chi Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper turns financial regulations into training data and a risk taxonomy, builds the first benchmark for financial compliance detection, and trains a smaller specialized model that beats larger general LLMs on compliance screening.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: General guard models focus on broad harm taxonomies and miss violations tied to concrete financial regulations, creating regulatory and consumer risk when LLMs are deployed in financial services.

Method: The paper builds a pipeline that reads financial regulations directly, induces a compliance risk taxonomy without predefined categories, synthesizes regulation-grounded training data, constructs FinGuard-Bench with expert annotations at query and response levels, and trains FinGuard on Qwen3-8B using supervised fine-tuning plus self-play reinforcement learning.

Result: FinGuard-Bench is introduced as the first benchmark for financial regulatory compliance detection, and FinGuard significantly outperforms dedicated guard models and larger general-purpose LLMs including Qwen3.5-397B-A17B and GPT-5.1 on this benchmark, while maintaining general safety and adapting to unseen policy documents.

Conclusion: A regulation-driven approach can produce effective financial compliance guard models directly from regulatory documents, and the resulting FinGuard model achieves state-of-the-art compliance detection while retaining general safety and adapting to institution-specific policies.

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in financial services, a single non-compliant interaction can expose institutions to regulatory penalties and direct consumer harm. Existing guard models are built around general harm taxonomies and overlook violations grounded in specific financial regulations. We address this gap with a regulation-driven pipeline that operates directly on regulatory documents, inducing a financial compliance risk taxonomy and synthesizing grounded training data without any predefined violation categories. Instantiating the pipeline on Chinese financial regulations, we release \textbf{FinGuard-Bench}, to our knowledge the first benchmark for financial regulatory compliance detection, with expert-annotated labels at both the query and response levels. We further train \textbf{FinGuard}, a financial compliance detection model built on Qwen3-8B and trained on the regulation-grounded data via supervised fine-tuning and self-play reinforcement learning. On FinGuard-Bench, FinGuard substantially outperforms all baselines, including dedicated guard models and much larger general-purpose LLMs such as Qwen3.5-397B-A17B and GPT-5.1. Furthermore, FinGuard also preserves general safety capabilities and adapts to unseen institution-specific policies using policy documents alone. We will publicly release the code, prompts, and resources used in this work on GitHub.

</details>


### [50] [PhoneWorld: Scaling Phone-Use Agent Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29486)
*Zhengyang Tang,Yuxuan Liu,Xin Lai,Junyi Li,Pengyuan Lyu,Jason,Yiduo Guo,Zhengyao Fang,Yang Ding,Yi Zhang,Weinong Wang,Huawen Shen,Xingran Zhou,Liang Wu,Fei Tang,Sunqi Fan,Shangpin Peng,Zheng Ruan,Anran Zhang,Benyou Wang,Rui Yan,Ji-Rong Wen,Chengquan Zhang,Han Hu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: PhoneWorld is a scalable pipeline that turns real mobile usage traces into runnable mock phone environments, tasks, verifiers, and training data, and it significantly improves mobile-agent performance across multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing mobile-agent benchmarks help evaluation, but they do not offer a scalable way to build many new realistic, controllable, and reproducible phone-use environments. This limits progress for phone-use agents.

Method: The paper converts real GUI trajectories and screenshots into mock Android apps by recovering important screens, screen transitions, state-changing interactions, and automatically verifiable user goals. It then derives executable tasks, rule-based verifiers, and training rollouts from these reconstructed environments.

Result: PhoneWorld currently covers 34 apps across 16 domains. Under a fixed training budget, replacing 10K steps of auxiliary AndroidWorld data with PhoneWorld supervision improves HYMobileBench by 17.7, AndroidControl by 6.0, AndroidWorld by 14.7, and PhoneWorld by 52.5 points. More PhoneWorld supervision further improves performance, and broader app coverage yields even larger gains under a fixed budget.

Conclusion: PhoneWorld provides a scalable, reusable pipeline for turning real mobile GUI trajectories into controllable phone-use environments, executable tasks, automatic verifiers, and training rollouts, shifting the field from hand-building individual benchmarks to scaling environment supply.

Abstract: A central bottleneck for phone-use agents is that controllable, reproducible environments covering real mobile behavior are hard to build at scale. Existing mobile-agent benchmarks have made important progress on evaluation, but they do not by themselves provide a scalable way to construct many new phone-use environments. We present PhoneWorld, a reusable pipeline that converts real GUI trajectories and screenshots into controllable phone-use environments, executable tasks, automatic verifiers, and training rollouts. Rather than hand-building one mobile benchmark at a time, PhoneWorld uses real trajectories to recover which screens matter, how screens connect, which interactions must change environment state, and which user goals admit automatic verification. From these signals, it builds runnable mock Android apps backed by read-only app content and mutable state, then derives executable tasks, rule-based verifiers, and training rollouts from the same environments. In its current instantiation, PhoneWorld covers 34 apps across 16 domains, spanning common consumer mobile behaviors such as search, browsing, shopping, booking, media, and social interaction. Under a fixed training budget, replacing 10K steps from an auxiliary AndroidWorld corpus in an AndroidWorld-based baseline with broad PhoneWorld supervision improves all four evaluation benchmarks at once, raising HYMobileBench by 17.7 points, AndroidControl by 6.0 points, AndroidWorld by 14.7 points, and PhoneWorld by 52.5 points. We then study two additional scaling questions: increasing the amount of PhoneWorld supervision strongly improves PhoneWorld performance, and under a fixed PhoneWorld budget, expanding app coverage yields even larger gains. Overall, PhoneWorld shifts the focus from building one mobile benchmark at a time to scaling the supply of phone-use environments themselves.

</details>


### [51] [On Asymmetric Optimization of Reasoning and Perception in Vision-Language Model Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29496)
*Xueqing Wu,Yu-Chi Lin,Kai-Wei Chang,Nanyun Peng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper shows that post-training helps reasoning much more than perception in vision-language models. It diagnoses why this happens in SFT and RL, and demonstrates simple fixes that significantly improve overall visual reasoning accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is that post-training has significantly improved reasoning in frontier vision-language models, but perception gains remain limited. This mismatch restricts end-to-end visual reasoning, so the paper aims to diagnose why perception lags behind reasoning and how to correct that imbalance.

Method: The authors build a controlled diagnostic framework with two synthetic tasks designed to separate perception from reasoning. They analyze post-training under supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning, then test interventions: dynamic loss reweighting for SFT and perception-aware reward design, including surrogate rewards, for RL.

Result: The study finds a consistent perception-reasoning asymmetry. In SFT, chain-of-thought token imbalance weakens perception learning; dynamic loss reweighting improves end-to-end performance by up to 18.2. In RL, reward coupling favors reasoning over perception; perception-aware rewards improve accuracy by up to 6.0, and surrogate perception rewards still yield gains of 3.2 points.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that post-training in vision-language models optimizes reasoning more effectively than perception, creating an asymmetric improvement bottleneck. It identifies different causes for this asymmetry in SFT and RL, and shows that targeted balancing methods can substantially improve end-to-end visual reasoning performance.

Abstract: Post-training has greatly improved reasoning in frontier vision-language models, yet its gains for perception remain comparatively limited, creating a bottleneck for end-to-end visual reasoning. To investigate this gap, we introduce a controlled diagnostic framework with two synthetic tasks that disentangle perception from reasoning. Our analysis reveals a consistent perception-reasoning asymmetry: posttraining improves reasoning more substantially than perception, though the underlying mechanism differs by training paradigm. For supervised fine-tuning (SFT), this asymmetry stems from token imbalance in chain-of-thought supervision, where perception occupies fewer tokens and thus receives a weaker training signal. Dynamically reweighting the loss mitigates this imbalance and boosts end-to-end performance by up to 18.2. For reinforcement learning (RL), the asymmetry instead arises from reward coupling: outcome rewards correlate more strongly with reasoning than with perception, weakening the signal for perception learning. Adding a perception-aware reward alleviates the imbalance and improves end-to-end accuracy by up to 6.0; even without groundtruth perception rewards, a reliable surrogate reward provide useful signal, yielding gains of 3.2 points. Together, our results comprehensively diagnose asymmetric optimization and suggest concrete interventions to balance perception and reasoning.

</details>


### [52] [Mask the Target: A Plug-and-Play Regularizer Against LoRA Forgetting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29498)
*Runze Xu,Arpit Garg,Hemanth Saratchandran,Simon Lucey*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LoRA fine-tuning can make LLMs forget old skills, especially on shifted data. This paper proposes a simple KL-based regularizer on non-target output tokens that needs no replay data or model changes, and it consistently improves the balance between learning new tasks and retaining prior abilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although LoRA often improves target-task performance, it can cause forgetting of previously learned capabilities, particularly under distribution shift. In practice, original pretraining and alignment data are usually unavailable, so the authors seek a simple way to reduce forgetting without replay data, architecture changes, or inference overhead.

Method: The paper studies LoRA adaptation in a replay-free setting and adds a loss-level regularizer: remove the ground-truth token from both base and adapted model output distributions, renormalize the remaining vocabulary probabilities, and apply KL divergence only on the non-target token distribution. This preserves the base model’s relative preferences among alternative tokens without directly conflicting with cross-entropy training.
