Our finding that edits suppress rather than overwrite knowledge explains why ROME and MEMIT fail to propagate changes to related facts. The identified common functional subspace informs detection and defense against unwanted edits.

</details>


### [111] [Self-Play Reinforcement Learning under Imperfect Information in Big 2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28863)
*Aalok Patwa*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper uses Big 2 as a four-player imperfect-information benchmark and shows that, with a fair comparison setup, PPO beats several value-based RL methods; moderate entropy and current-policy self-play further improve performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to evaluate how RL agents handle hidden information, sparse and delayed rewards, non-stationary opponents, multiplayer interaction, and variable action spaces in a controlled but challenging domain.

Method: The paper builds a self-play reinforcement learning framework for the four-player card game Big 2, allowing controlled comparison of policy-gradient and value-based agents under the same environment, state/action representation, training budget, and evaluation protocol. It compares PPO with Monte Carlo Q approximation, SARSA, and Q-learning, and studies entropy regularization and different self-play curricula.

Result: PPO outperforms Monte Carlo Q approximation, SARSA, and Q-learning against random, greedy, and heuristic opponents. Moderate entropy regularization improves PPO by avoiding overly deterministic policies, and current-policy self-play yields a stronger finite-budget training curriculum than checkpoint self-play or fixed-opponent training.

Conclusion: Big 2 is a useful controlled benchmark for studying deep reinforcement learning in imperfect-information multiplayer settings, and under matched experimental conditions PPO is the strongest method among the compared baselines.

Abstract: Imperfect-information multiplayer games test whether agents can act under hidden information, sparse rewards, and non-stationary opponents. We study these challenges in Big 2, a four-player imperfect-information card game. We develop a self-play RL framework for Big 2 that enables controlled comparisons between policy-gradient and value-approximating agents. Under a common environment, input representation, training budget, and evaluation protocol, PPO outperforms Monte Carlo Q approximation, SARSA, and Q-learning against random, greedy, and heuristic Big 2 opponents. We further find that moderate entropy regularization improves PPO by preventing the policy from becoming overly deterministic, and that current-policy self-play provides a stronger finite-budget curriculum than checkpoint self-play or fixed-opponent training. Together, these results show that Big 2 is a useful controlled setting for studying deep RL under imperfect information, multiplayer interaction, delayed rewards, and variable action sets.

</details>


### [112] [Continuity and Ordinality Matter: Constraining Time Series Tokens for Effective Time Series Analysis with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28866)
*Musheng Li,Ziying Zhang,Cheng jin,Yuantao Gu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Token-based TS-LLMs work better when token embeddings respect the natural smoothness and order of time series. COM adds geometric constraints at initialization and training time, leading to consistent benchmark improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior token-based time series LLMs ignore the inherent continuity and ordinality of time series tokens, which weakens representation quality and limits downstream performance.

Method: The paper proposes COM (Continuity and Ordinality Matter), which injects continuity- and ordinality-aware geometric constraints into both embedding initialization and model training for token-based TS-LLMs.

Result: Experiments on multiple time series analysis benchmarks show consistent gains over prior token-based TS-LLMs, with competitive accuracy and strong generalizability.

Conclusion: COM argues that preserving continuity and ordinality in token embeddings is crucial for token-based time series LLMs, and its geometric constraints improve performance and generalization across benchmarks.

Abstract: Token-based time series large language models (TS-LLMs) have emerged as a promising direction for time series analysis and reasoning. However, prior studies largely overlook the inherent continuity and ordinality of time series tokens, which substantially limits model performance. In this paper, we argue that preserving these properties in time series token embeddings is crucial for the effectiveness of token-based TS-LLMs. To this end, we propose COM (Continuity and Ordinality Matter), a continuity- and ordinality-aware strategy that integrates geometric constraints into both the initialization and training stages. Empirical results on multiple time series analysis benchmarks demonstrate that COM consistently improves the performance of token-based TS-LLMs, achieving competitive results and strong generalizability. Code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/COM .

</details>


### [113] [FedQHD: Closed-Form Function-Space Federated Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29002)
*Yuchen Hou,Yongshan Chen,Zhuowen Zou,Calvin Yeung,Mohsen Imani,Tian Lan,Mahdi Imani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedQHD replaces inconsistent parameter averaging in federated Q-learning with linear-readout aggregation over random-feature encoders, giving exact averaging when encoders are shared and efficient teacher projection when they differ; theory characterizes the resulting federation gap, and experiments show better or comparable performance at lower compute.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing FedAvg-style federated reinforcement learning methods average parameters, but parameter averaging is generally not function-space consistent for nonlinear networks or heterogeneous encoders. As a result, the aggregated model may not represent the weighted average of client value functions. The work is motivated by the need for a federated RL method that preserves nonlinear state representation while allowing principled, closed-form, and computationally efficient function aggregation across heterogeneous clients.

Method: The paper proposes FedQHD, where each client represents Q-functions using a nonlinear hyperdimensional/random-feature state encoder and a linear trainable readout matrix. In the shared-encoder case, global aggregation is done by weighted averaging of local readout matrices, which exactly matches function-space averaging. In the heterogeneous-encoder case, the server averages client Q-values on a shared anchor-state set to build a global teacher, and each client projects this teacher into its own representation using a single ridge regression step. The paper also analyzes the federation gap relative to an oracle projection.

Result: Theoretical results show that the federation gap decomposes into three sources: subspace misalignment, anchor-set conditioning, and regularization bias. The analysis identifies the regime m >= D_i as well-conditioned, where the gap becomes proportional to the encoder heterogeneity floor. Empirically, on four continuous-state, discrete-action control benchmarks, FedQHD matches or outperforms FedAvg-based baselines and distillation alternatives while using substantially less computation. The observed relationship between federation gap and encoder dimension is consistent with theory.

Conclusion: FedQHD provides a function-space consistent federated Q-learning framework by combining hyperdimensional/random-feature encoders with linear readouts. It enables exact aggregation under a shared encoder and an efficient teacher-compilation mechanism under heterogeneous encoders, with theory explaining the federation gap and experiments showing competitive or better performance with lower computation.

Abstract: Federated reinforcement learning enables decentralized agents to collaboratively improve policies or value estimates without exchanging raw trajectories. However, FedAvg-style parameter averaging is not function-space consistent: when clients use heterogeneous encoders or even identical nonlinear networks, averaged parameters need not correspond to the weighted average of client value functions in any common function space. We propose FedQHD, a federated Q-learning method using hyperdimensional (random-feature) state encoders with a linear readout, so that Q-functions are nonlinear in state yet linear in trainable parameters. This linear structure enables closed-form aggregation. With a shared encoder, the function-space consensus update coincides exactly with weighted averaging of local readout matrices. With heterogeneous encoders, the server constructs a global teacher by averaging client Q-values on a shared anchor-state set, and each client compiles this teacher into its local representation via a single ridge projection. We formalize the federation gap -- the error incurred when compiling a federated teacher into a heterogeneous client representation -- relative to a client-specific oracle projection. We show that this gap decomposes into subspace misalignment, anchor-set conditioning, and regularization bias. We further identify the anchor-to-dimension ratio $m \geq D_i$ as the well-conditioned regime in which the gap reduces to a multiple of the encoder heterogeneity floor. On four continuous-state, discrete-action control benchmarks, FedQHD matches or outperforms FedAvg-style baselines and distillation-based alternatives while requiring substantially less computation, and the empirical dependence of the federation gap on encoder dimension matches our theoretical analysis.

</details>


### [114] [Pre-Registering the Detectable Effect: A Paired-MDE Budget for 4-bit Quantization Benchmarks, with a Pilot Audit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28873)
*Zexin Zhuang,Yanhang Li,Zhichao Fan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper gives a one-line conservative MDE formula for planning quantization benchmarks, shows that observed NF4-vs-FP16 differences in a small pilot are mostly too small to distinguish from expected noise under reasonable assumptions, and argues that prompt-template variance must be controlled because it can be larger than the quantization effect itself.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to turn vague questions about the reliability of quantization benchmark claims into a simple ex ante planning rule that benchmark designers can commit to before running experiments. The work also addresses confusion between true quantization effects, benchmark sampling noise, and prompt-template noise, especially in small-sample settings such as n=100 subsamples.

Method: The paper adapts the classical Miettinen (1968) paired-binary sample-size calculation to quantization benchmarking and derives a conservative MDE bound: δ* ≤ (z_{1-α/2}+z_{1-β})√(ρ_d/m), where m is the paired item count and ρ_d is the FP16-NF4 disagreement rate. It illustrates the bound using an unpaired pilot audit across four models and four benchmarks with k=5 splits of n=100, and adds a parallel MMLU prompt-template sensitivity study to compare quantization-noise scale with prompt-template noise. It also proposes a short five-line pre-registration template for benchmark planning.

Result: Assuming a planning value of ρ_d=0.10, every observed NF4-FP16 delta falls below the implied MDE. Most cross-split standard deviations are within ±1.5 percentage points of the binomial reference √(p(1-p)/n), suggesting reported instability is largely explained by binomial sampling noise. One borderline case, OPT-WinoGrande with |Δ|=3.2 pp, remains below the MDE at ρ_d=0.10 but exceeds it at ρ_d=0.05, highlighting the planning trade-off. On MMLU, prompt-template ranges of 2–10 pp match or exceed the largest observed quantization delta, indicating prompt-template variance can dominate quantization audits.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that a conservative minimum detectable effect bound can be adapted from paired-binary sample-size theory to quantization benchmarks, making quantization-claim reliability plannable in advance. In the pilot audit, all observed NF4-FP16 differences were below the implied MDE under the assumed disagreement rate ρ_d=0.10, and much of the variance on n=100 benchmark splits appears to be ordinary binomial sampling noise rather than deeper benchmark unreliability. It also concludes that prompt-template variation on MMLU can be as large as or larger than quantization effects, so prompt templates should be fixed before auditing quantization.

Abstract: This is a planning-method note with an unpaired pilot audit. We adapt the classical paired-binary sample-size calculation (Miettinen, 1968) to quantization benchmarks, giving a conservative minimum detectable effect (MDE) bound $δ^{*} \le (z_{1-α/2}+z_{1-β})\sqrt{ρ_d/m}$ in the paired item count $m$ and the FP16-NF4 disagreement rate $ρ_d$. The bound turns "how reliable is my quantization claim?" into a one-line budget a benchmark designer can commit to before running. We illustrate the bound on four models and four benchmarks ($k=5$ splits of $n=100$), and add a parallel MMLU prompt-template study to put the bound's quantization-noise scale alongside the prompt-noise scale. Assuming $ρ_d=0.10$ (an unmeasured planning value), all observed NF4-FP16 deltas fall below the implied MDE, and most cross-split SDs lie within $\pm 1.5$ pp of the binomial reference $\sqrt{p(1-p)/n}$, so much of the variance reported as "benchmark unreliability" on $n=100$ subsamples is binomial sampling noise. The single borderline cell (OPT-WinoGrande, $|Δ|=3.2$ pp) is below the implied MDE at $ρ_d=0.10$ but above it at $ρ_d=0.05$, illustrating the planning trade-off the bound makes explicit. On MMLU, prompt-template ranges of 2-10 pp meet or exceed the largest observed quantization delta (3.2 pp), so a quantization audit that does not first fix the prompt template absorbs template variance into its noise floor. We complement the bound with a five-line pre-registration template.

</details>


### [115] [Sequential Physics-Constrained Neural Operator Forward Modeling for the $\textit{Norne}$ Reservoir System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28909)
*Clement Etienam,Juntao Yang,Oleg Ovcharenko,Nick Luiken,Tsubasa Onishi,Nefeli Moridis,Issam Said*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A theory-backed PINO surrogate for the Norne reservoir stays stable over long autoregressive rollouts, matches simulator outputs well, and runs roughly ten thousand times faster than the baseline simulator.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to replace or augment expensive finite-volume reservoir simulation with fast surrogate models that remain accurate over long sequential rollouts. A key challenge is that autoregressive prediction suffers from covariate shift, instability, and training bias, especially in high-dimensional multiphase flow systems. The paper addresses these issues with theory-guided neural-operator design and training.

Method: The paper builds a mathematical and computational framework for autoregressive surrogate modeling of reservoir flow using FNO and PINO. It formulates the dynamics in product Sobolev spaces, analyzes covariate shift under sequential rollout, studies physics-constrained spectral stability of the learned timestep map, and derives truncated backpropagation-through-time and Adam optimization error/convergence results. Empirically, it trains and evaluates the models on the Norne benchmark reservoir.

Result: The main results are: well-posedness and local Lipschitz bounds for the implicit timestep map; a Wasserstein-2 covariate-shift bound growing like ε(L^n-1)/(L-1); a PINO stability result showing the learned Jacobian spectral radius can be reduced to ρ_F + Cλ_R^{-1/2}, implying uniform-in-time rollout error bounded by ε/(1-ρ); TBPTT bias decays geometrically as O(ρ^K) with an optimal window scaling logarithmically in T/σ^2; Adam convergence scales as O(1/sqrt(t)) + O(ρ^{K*}). Empirically, the surrogate achieves R^2 above 0.99 for oil, above 0.90 for gas, about 0.80 for pressure, and improving water accuracy across the full horizon, with ~10^4x speedup.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that sequential neural-operator surrogates, especially physics-informed Fourier Neural Operators, can model large-scale three-phase black-oil reservoir dynamics with both theoretical guarantees and strong long-horizon accuracy, while delivering orders-of-magnitude speedup over conventional simulators.

Abstract: We develop a comprehensive mathematical and computational framework for sequential surrogate modeling of three-phase black-oil reservoir dynamics using neural operators, with particular emphasis on Fourier Neural Operators (FNO) and their physics-informed variant (PINO). The application focus is the Norne benchmark reservoir, defined on a heterogeneous $46\times112\times22$ grid ($N=113,344$ cells), with a production history spanning $T=30$ timesteps covering 3298 days. Our theoretical contributions are organized around four interlocking problems: (1) functional-analytic formulation in a product-Sobolev-space setting, including well-posedness of the implicit timestep map and sharp local Lipschitz estimates; (2) covariate shift quantification, proving that the Wasserstein-2 distance grows as $W_2 \leq \varepsilon(L^n-1)/(L-1)$, with exponential population-risk discrepancy for $L>1$; (3) physics-constrained spectral stability, showing PINO training with $λ_R \geq λ^*_R$ reduces the learned Jacobian spectral radius to $ρ_F + Cλ_R^{-1/2}$, yielding uniform-in-time rollout error $|δ_n| \leq \varepsilon/(1-ρ)$; and (4) $K$-step TBPTT gradient analysis, deriving geometric bias decay $O(ρ^K)$, optimal window $K^ = O(\log(T/σ^2))$, and Adam convergence $O(1/\sqrt{t}) + O(ρ^{K^*})$. Empirical validation confirms all theoretical predictions: autoregressive PINO surrogates sustain $R^2>0.99$ (oil), $R^2>0.90$ (gas), $R^2\approx 0.80$ (pressure), and monotonically improving $R^2$ (water) across the full 3298-day horizon, trained on eight NVIDIA B200 GPUs in under one hour. A 1000-member ensemble runs in under one minute on a single B200 GPU, giving a ${\sim}10^4\times$ wall-clock speedup over the OPM finite-volume simulator.

</details>


### [116] [FormInv: A Measurement Protocol for Semantic Invariance in Mathematical Reasoning Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29001)
*Nishal Thomas,Noel Thomas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper argues that theorem benchmark rankings are fragile: bad paraphrases and benchmark-family choices can change who 'wins,' while accuracy alone misses large semantic-instability gaps. It proposes FormInv, a low-cost audit and invariance-evaluation framework using SCR, Cochran's Q, and cross-model agreement to detect these problems and support better model selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard benchmark accuracy hides whether models give stable answers under semantically equivalent restatements, and benchmark rankings may be skewed by flawed paraphrases or by arbitrary choices of paraphrase families. The work aims to expose and quantify this hidden evaluation instability.

Method: The paper introduces FormInv, which audits paraphrase quality using cross-model unanimity, evaluates 9 frontier models on Lean4-verified theorem datasets across multiple paraphrase families, measures Semantic Consistency Rate (SCR) and per-theorem Cochran's Q, proves a No-Free-Benchmark corollary about ranking manipulability via family weighting, and proposes FormInvSelector for regime-aware model selection.

Result: A MathCheck audit found 4 incorrect paraphrases in 129 groups, enough to change GPT-4o's rank from 2nd to 4th and move Claude Haiku and DeepSeek V3 above it. The cross-model unanimity protocol detected these errors cheaply and achieved 100% recall on external benchmarks. In the authors' own dataset, 47% of auto-generated connective-variation paraphrases were semantically incorrect. Across 9 models, accuracy ranged narrowly from 86% to 96%, but SCR ranged widely from 50% to 82%, revealing a 32-point stability gap hidden by standard metrics.

Conclusion: Current theorem-proving benchmarks can be materially distorted by semantically incorrect paraphrases and by benchmark-family weighting choices; therefore benchmark evaluation should include paraphrase auditing and invariance-based metrics such as SCR and Cochran's Q, rather than relying on aggregate accuracy alone.

Abstract: A paraphrase-quality audit of MathCheck (ICLR 2025) detected 4 semantically incorrect paraphrases in 129 groups (3.1%); removing them drops GPT-4o from rank 2 to rank 4 and elevates Claude Haiku and DeepSeek V3 above it; these ranking changes are invisible to any single-model evaluation. Cross-model unanimity found these errors automatically (>= 3/4 models for MathCheck; >= 6/9 for our primary evaluation) for under $10; in our own dataset the same protocol found that 47% of auto-generated connective-variation paraphrases were semantically incorrect. That flaw compounds a deeper measurement gap: Claude Haiku 4.5 achieves 86% accuracy yet SCR=50%, meaning half its theorems are answered differently under semantically equivalent restatements, while aggregate accuracy across 9 models spans only 86-96% yet Semantic Consistency Rates (SCR) span 50-82% -- a 32-point gap invisible to standard benchmarks. Formally, for any target ranking over 9 frontier models there exists a weighting over paraphrase families that realizes it (No-Free-Benchmark corollary), because no model Pareto-dominates all families -- so benchmark designers who select families are implicitly choosing which model wins. FormInv supplies the audit protocol (replicated on external benchmarks at 100% recall), SCR and per-theorem Cochran's Q as primary invariance measures evaluated on 9 models across 366-811 items (on Lean4-verified theorems), and FormInvSelector for regime-aware model selection.

</details>


### [117] [Moment Matching Q-Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29033)
*Yiyan,Liang,Sifei Liu,Weitong Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MoMa QL uses MMD-based full-moment matching to remove the inference bottleneck of score/flow-based RL policies, keeping performance competitive while making training and deployment faster, especially for offline-to-online RL.
MoMa QL speeds up score/flow-based reinforcement learning by matching policy distributions with MMD, delivering stable learning, competitive benchmark performance, and better online finetuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Score-based and flow-based generative models are expressive and useful in RL, but their inference is slow because action sampling often requires iterative procedures. This latency becomes a serious computational bottleneck, especially in reinforcement learning settings that repeatedly sample actions and need fast online adaptation.
Although score-based and flow-based models are powerful, their iterative sampling causes high inference latency, which limits practical efficiency in RL, particularly for iterative and online settings.

Method: The paper proposes Moment Matching Q-Learning (MoMa QL), a framework that applies maximum mean discrepancy (MMD) as a strong regularizer to match all-order moments between the original policy distribution and the target distribution. This encourages distribution-level convergence for conditional score functions and avoids the need for prolonged iterative sampling during action generation.
The method introduces MMD-based moment matching into Q-learning for score-based and flow-based generative policies, enforcing alignment of full distribution statistics rather than relying on slow iterative inference.

Result: Empirically, MoMa QL achieves higher computational efficiency while maintaining comparable or competitive returns on multiple D4RL benchmarks. It also shows superior performance in offline-to-online RL, where faster action sampling enables stronger and quicker online finetuning.
Experiments show that MoMa QL is more efficient than prior score/flow-based RL approaches, performs competitively on D4RL tasks, and is especially strong in offline-to-online RL due to faster policy adaptation.

Conclusion: MoMa QL replaces expensive iterative sampling in score/flow-based RL with MMD-based moment matching, yielding stabler distribution-level learning and substantially better efficiency while preserving strong task performance, especially in offline-to-online finetuning.
MoMa QL uses maximum mean discrepancy to align the policy distribution with the target distribution, reducing inference cost of score/flow-based policies and improving adaptability in RL.

Abstract: Score-based and flow-based generative models exhibit remarkable expressive capacity in capturing complex distributions, and have been extensively deployed in tasks ranging from image generation to reinforcement learning. Nevertheless, these models suffer from prolonged inference latency, which imposes a significant computational bottleneck in RL with iterative sampling. To overcome this limitation, we propose a new framework named Moment Matching Q-Learning (MoMa QL), which utilizes a technique from statistical hypothesis testing known as maximum mean discrepancy (MMD) that intend to match all orders of statistics between the original and target distribution. By enforcing strong regularization on all moment statistics, this algorithm guarantees distribution-level convergence for conditional score function and remains stable under various hyperparameters. Empirically, we show that our method MoMa QL is more computationally efficient with a comparable if not competitive performance in various D4RL tasks. Remarkably, by accelerating the action sampling process for flow-based policies, MoMa QL demonstrates superior performance in offline-to-online RL tasks because of faster and stronger adaptability for online interactive finetuning.

</details>


### [118] [Parallel Adaptive Multi-Objective Evolutionary Learning of Discretized Bayesian Network Classifiers for Clinical Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29058)
*Damy M. F. Ha,Tanja Alderliesten,Peter A. N. Bosman*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper makes Baymex faster and more practical for real clinical data by parallelizing its Bayesian network search and reducing overfitting. The result is a set of compact, interpretable Bayesian network classifiers that are as good as or better than standard clinical models, with major runtime speedups.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although Bayesian networks are attractive for explainable AI and Baymex already outperformed prior BN learning methods, the original approach was limited by high computational cost and evaluation only on synthetic data. The work aims to improve scalability and validate Baymex on real-world clinical classification tasks.

Method: The paper extends Baymex, a multi-objective evolutionary algorithm for learning discretized Bayesian networks, by adding a parallelization strategy and an adaptive mechanism to steer optimization away from overfitting. It is reconfigured to train Bayesian network classifiers by jointly optimizing cross-entropy loss and the BIC complexity term.

Result: The improved method achieved speedups of more than 54x on a 16-core CPU. On RADCURE, SUPPORT, and one in-house clinical dataset, Baymex matched or outperformed decision trees, logistic regression, naive Bayes, and random forests, while producing compact and clinically inspectable Bayesian networks. It also identified multiple plausible classifiers aligned with known clinical predictors.

Conclusion: The enhanced Baymex framework can learn compact, clinically interpretable Bayesian network classifiers on real-world datasets, achieving statistically similar or better predictive performance than common clinical baselines while greatly improving computational scalability through parallelization.

Abstract: Bayesian Networks (BNs) are of interest from an explainable AI viewpoint, offering transparent probabilistic models for decision support. Baymex is a recently introduced multi-objective evolutionary algorithm for learning discretized BNs, enabling experts to trade-off different objectives of interest, such as likelihood, model complexity, and prior beliefs. While Baymex has been shown to outperform state-of-the-art BN learning approaches, Baymex still 1) requires a lot of computation time and 2) has only been evaluated on synthetic data. To improve scalability, we introduce a parallelization strategy as well as a mechanism that enables adaptively steering optimization toward networks that overfit less. We furthermore reconfigure Baymex to train a BN classifier through multi-objective optimization of cross-entropy loss and the BIC complexity term so as to evaluate its performance on real-world clinical classification tasks. Besides observing speedups up to over 54 times on a 16-core CPU, comparisons against clinically familiar baselines (decision trees, logistic regression, naive Bayes, and random forests) on two open-source (RADCURE and SUPPORT) and one in-house dataset, show that Baymex obtains statistically similar or better predictive performance while producing compact, clinically inspectable BNs. Importantly, Baymex finds multiple plausible BN classifiers that contain predictors consistent with established clinical factors.

</details>


### [119] [Do Deep Networks Forget Initialization? A Forgetting-Time View of Practical Inductive Bias](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29152)
*Mohua Das,Pierfrancesco Beneventano,Shibshankar Dey,Gareth H. McKinkey,Tomaso Poggio*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper shows that the trained model’s inductive bias is not just the architecture’s random-init prior, but that prior after training has either preserved or erased it. Low-LR SGD can preserve strong initialization memory even at full training accuracy, while Adam and stronger regularization tend to erase it.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper asks how much of the bias induced by random initialization survives after training, and introduces a measurable notion of initialization memory to quantify this effect.

Method: Controlled CIFAR-10 experiments on ResNet models, measuring 'initialization memory' as the dependence of validation-selected predictors on the scale of random initialization across optimizers, learning rates, batch sizes, training durations, and explicit norm control.

Result: Low-learning-rate SGD can achieve near-perfect training accuracy while still strongly remembering initialization, with test accuracy varying by 26.5 percentage points across initialization scales on ResNet-9 at batch size 128. This persistence remains even after 5,000 epochs. Adam-family methods largely erase this dependence, and SGD can also forget when larger learning rates are combined with explicit L2 norm control. The findings suggest forgetting time scales are governed by explicit or implicit regularization.

Conclusion: Training does not simply preserve the architectural prior from random initialization; the effective inductive bias of a trained network is the initialization prior filtered by the forgetting dynamics of the optimization pipeline.

Abstract: Randomly initialized neural networks induce a prior over functions, but the predictor used in practice is produced only after training. We ask how much of this initial bias survives the training pipeline. To make the question measurable, we introduce initialization memory: the dependence of the validation-selected predictor on the scale of the random initialization. We perform controlled CIFAR-10 experiments on ResNets where initialization memory already sharply separates training regimes. Low-learning-rate SGD can interpolate while still remembering its initialization: on ResNet-9 with batch size $b=128$, test accuracy varies by $26.5$ percentage points across initialization scales despite $\ge99.5\%$ training accuracy. This is not undertraining: extending the same low-learning-rate regime to $5{,}000$ epochs leaves the spread essentially unchanged. In contrast, Adam-family methods largely erase the dependence. SGD can also be made to forget when larger learning rates are paired with explicit $L_2$ norm control. We interpret these findings in terms of the time scale of forgetting: gradient-flow-like dynamics can preserve initialization memory, whereas stochastic finite-step effects, explicit norm decay, and adaptive preconditioning erase it on scales governed by the size of explicit or implicit regularization. The practical inductive bias of a trained network is therefore not the architectural prior alone, but the architectural prior after being filtered by the forgetting dynamics of the training pipeline; and the same regularizers that improve generalization are precisely those that erase memory of initialization.

</details>


### [120] [Unveiling Multi-regime Patterns in SciML: Distinct Failure Modes and Regime-specific Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29153)
*Yuxin Wang,Yuanzhe Hu,Xiaokun Zhong,Xiaopeng Wang,Haiquan Lu,Tianyu Pang,Michael W. Mahoney,Yujun Yan,Pu Ren,Yaoqing Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SciML training is not governed by one universal pattern: it reliably splits into three regimes, each with different optimization behavior and failure characteristics, so robustness improvements should be regime-aware rather than one-size-fits-all.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SciML models often behave inconsistently under different hyperparameters, but existing analyses usually lack a unified way to explain these qualitatively different training behaviors and failure modes.

Method: The paper proposes a regime-aware diagnostic framework that jointly studies model performance, training dynamics, and loss-landscape geometry across different hyperparameter settings, models, constraint strategies, and optimizers.

Result: The study finds a robust three-regime structure across common SciML model families, shows that no single optimization method works best in all regimes, and identifies fine-grained failures that challenge conventional interpretations of loss-landscape metrics.

Conclusion: A regime-aware view is necessary for SciML training because models consistently separate into distinct training regimes, optimizer effectiveness depends on the regime, and standard loss-landscape metrics can miss important failure modes.

Abstract: Neural networks trained under different hyperparameter settings can fall into distinct training "regimes," with consistent behavior within regimes and qualitative differences across regimes. In this paper, we study such multi-regime behavior in scientific machine learning (SciML) models through a regime-aware diagnostic framework that jointly analyzes performance, training dynamics, and loss-landscape geometry. We identify three key findings: (i) a consistent three-regime structure emerges across many standard SciML models, different constraint enforcements, and various optimizer designs; (ii) optimization effectiveness is regime-specific, with no single method performing well across all regimes; and (iii) SciML models can exhibit fine-grained failure modes that can challenge conventional interpretations of standard loss-landscape metrics. Our results provide an approach to establish a unified, task-oblivious perspective on failure modes in SciML and to inform regime-aware guidance for improving robustness. We validate these findings across widely-used SciML models, including physics-informed neural networks, neural operators, and neural ordinary differential equations, on benchmarks spanning representative ordinary and partial differential equations.

</details>


### [121] [RUBRIC-ARROW: Alternating Pointwise Rubric Reward Modeling for LLM Post-training in Non-verifiable Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29156)
*Haoxiang Jiang,Zihan Dong,Tianci Liu,Wanying Wang,Ran Xu,Tony Yu,Linjun Zhang,Haoyu Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RUBRIC-ARROW alternates training a rubric generator and rubric-based judge using pairwise preferences, reduces tied scores with probabilistic scoring, avoids dependence on frontier LLMs, and improves both reward modeling and downstream LLM training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pointwise reward models are important for LLM post-training but are weak at absolute scoring in subjective, non-verifiable tasks. Existing rubric-based methods help by decomposing evaluation criteria, but they often depend on frontier LLMs and produce many ties due to hard Boolean aggregation.

Method: The paper proposes RUBRIC-ARROW, an alternating training framework with a rubric generator and a rubric-conditioned judge. It uses a probability-based scoring rule, phase-specific preference rewards, and alternating GRPO, trained in the RL stage only from pairwise preference data.

Result: Experiments show competitive reward-modeling accuracy and consistent improvements in downstream policy post-training.

Conclusion: RUBRIC-ARROW can train a strong pointwise evaluator without relying on frontier LLMs, improving reward-model accuracy and downstream policy post-training.

Abstract: Pointwise reward modeling offers critical signals for LLM post-training, yet struggles with absolute scoring in subjective, non-verifiable settings. Rubric-based methods address this by decomposing evaluation into explicit criteria, but existing approaches typically depend on frontier LLMs and suffer from ties caused by hard Boolean aggregation. We present RUBRIC-ARROW, an alternating framework that jointly trains a rubric generator and a rubric-conditioned judge, with its RL stage using only pairwise preference data. Our method couples a probability-based scoring rule that reduces ties with phase-specific preference-based rewards and an alternating GRPO scheme that together train the pointwise evaluator. Extensive experiments show that RUBRIC-ARROW achieves competitive reward-modeling accuracy and yields consistent gains for downstream policy post-training.

</details>


### [122] [Parallax: Parameterized Local Linear Attention for Language Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29157)
*Yifei Zuo,Dhruv Pai,Zhichen Zeng,Alec Dewulf,Shuming Hu,Zhaoran Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Parallax turns Local Linear Attention into a practical LLM attention layer by replacing its unstable solver with a learned covariance-probing mechanism, improving both efficiency and model quality over standard attention baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard softmax attention remains structurally dominant in LLMs despite its limitations. Prior efficient attention work often targets speed or memory, but Local Linear Attention offers a theoretically better local linear estimate than softmax’s local constant estimate. The challenge is that LLA has not been scalable in practice due to computational cost and numerical instability, motivating a new formulation suitable for large-scale LLM pretraining.

Method: The paper replaces the numerically unstable solver in Local Linear Attention with a learned parameterized mechanism. Parallax introduces an additional query-like projector to probe key-value covariance, situates the design within a broader family of attention mechanisms defined by bandwidth, probe construction, and affine structure, and develops a hardware-aware algorithm and decode kernel with higher arithmetic intensity than FlashAttention.

Result: Parallax’s prototype decode kernel matches or outperforms FlashAttention 2/3 across varied batch sizes and context lengths. In pretraining at 0.6B and 1.7B model sizes, it consistently improves perplexity and these gains transfer to downstream tasks. Ablations show that the optimizer Muon is important for fully realizing Parallax’s capacity, suggesting strong attention architecture-optimizer co-design effects.

Conclusion: Parallax is a scalable parameterized form of Local Linear Attention that improves the bias-variance tradeoff of attention-derived associative memory while remaining practical for LLM pretraining. It achieves consistent perplexity and downstream gains at 0.6B and 1.7B scales, provides a Pareto improvement under both parameter- and compute-matched settings, and benefits strongly from architecture-optimizer co-design with Muon.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have become the central paradigm in artificial intelligence, yet the core computational primitive of attention has remained structurally unchanged. Local Linear Attention (LLA) is an attention mechanism derived from nonparametric statistics in the test-time regression framework. In contrast to prior research on efficient attention variants, LLA upgrades the local constant estimate in softmax attention to a local linear estimate, yielding provably superior bias-variance tradeoffs for associative memory. However, LLA has not been scaled in LLM pretraining due to computational and numerical stability concerns. We introduce Parallax, a parameterized Local Linear Attention that is scalable for LLMs. Parallax eliminates the numerical solver in LLA and learns an extra query-like projector that probes the KV covariance. We place Parallax within a family of attention mechanisms connected by the bandwidth, the probe construction and the affine structure. We propose a hardware-aware algorithm that increases the arithmetic intensity over FlashAttention, shifting attention into a more compute bound regime. Our prototype decode kernel matches or outperforms FlashAttention 2/3 across diverse batch sizes and context lengths. We pretrain Parallax at 0.6B and 1.7B scales and find consistent perplexity improvements throughout pretraining with gains that transfer to downstream benchmarks. The advantage persists under both parameter-matched and compute-matched controls, demonstrating a Pareto improvement. We perform careful pretraining ablations and identify a novel phenomenon whereby Muon unlocks the capacity of Parallax. To our knowledge, this is the first empirical demonstration of strong architecture-optimizer codesign for attention mechanisms in the architecture research literature.

</details>


### [123] [PROTOCOL: Late Interaction Retrieval for Protein Homolog Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29158)
*Gabrielle Cohn,Rohan Gumaste,Minh Hoang,Vihan Lakshman*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ProtoCol improves remote homology search by comparing proteins at the residue-embedding level with late interaction instead of collapsing each protein into one vector, leading to better retrieval on standard benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical alignment methods lose sensitivity for remote homology detection when global sequence similarity is weak, and prior embedding-based retrieval methods often compress proteins into a single vector, which can hide important local motifs, domains, and conserved residues.

Method: ProtoCol represents each protein as a set of residue embeddings from a protein language model and applies a ColBERT-style late interaction scoring scheme. Proteins are encoded independently, candidate embeddings are pre-computable, and retrieval scores are computed using MaxSim over residue embeddings.

Result: On SCOPe superfamily and Pfam clan benchmarks, ProtoCol outperforms sequence-composition methods, alignment-based methods, pooled protein language model embeddings, and trained single-vector baselines in homolog retrieval.

Conclusion: ProtoCol demonstrates that residue-level late interaction is an effective retrieval layer for remote protein homology search, especially in the low-similarity 'twilight zone', outperforming several established baselines.

Abstract: Protein homology search underlies function annotation, structure prediction, and evolutionary analysis, but remains challenging in the "twilight zone," where global sequence similarity is weak and classical alignment methods lose sensitivity. Protein language models provide context-aware representations that could improve alignment sensitivity in this regime. However, prior protein embedding-based retrieval pipelines often pool these representations into a single vector, potentially obscuring local motifs, domains, or conserved residues that reveal remote homology. We introduce ProtoCol, a model which represents proteins as sets of residue embeddings and uses ColBERT-style late interaction to test whether residue-level comparison improves homolog retrieval. ProtoCol encodes proteins independently, keeps candidate representations pre-computable, and scores candidates with MaxSim over residue embeddings. On SCOPe superfamily and Pfam clan benchmarks, ProtoCol outperforms sequence-composition, alignment-based, pooled PLM, and trained single-vector baselines, supporting late interaction as an effective retrieval layer for remote homology search.

</details>


### [124] [Probabilistic bias adjustment of seasonal forecasts using generative machine learning: A case study of Arctic sea ice predictions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29172)
*Parsa Gooya,Reinel Sospedra-Alfonso*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper upgrades a cVAE-based post-processing system for seasonal Arctic sea-ice forecasts by using a generator, CRPS loss, and higher-resolution targets. The new model produces large, bias-corrected probabilistic ensembles that are more accurate, better calibrated, less blurry, and richer in fine-scale detail than standard approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Seasonal climate forecasting needs large, reliable probabilistic ensembles, but running high-resolution large ensembles directly is computationally expensive. Existing forecast systems also suffer from drift, bias, and growing spatio-temporal errors with lead time, and prior cVAE post-processing methods tend to lose fine-scale energy and produce blurry outputs. The paper is motivated by the need for a computationally efficient post-processing method that both calibrates forecasts and preserves or enhances spatial detail.

Method: The authors extend an existing cVAE-based probabilistic post-processing framework for seasonal Arctic sea-ice prediction. The new approach conditions on biased raw model forecasts, uses a generator instead of a Gaussian-parameterized decoder to reduce blur, replaces the MSE reconstruction objective with Continuous Ranked Probability Score (CRPS) to better optimize probabilistic forecast quality, and learns from a higher-resolution observational target than the raw forecast input.

Result: The proposed model generates large ensembles of bias-adjusted Arctic sea-ice forecasts that outperform benchmark predictions. Compared with the raw forecast and the standard cVAE, the method yields better calibration, greater consistency with the observed distribution, smaller forecast errors, improved sharpness, enhanced effective resolution, and stronger spectral power at fine scales.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that replacing the standard Gaussian decoder in a conditional VAE with a generator, optimizing with CRPS instead of MSE, and training against a higher-resolution target improves probabilistic post-processing of seasonal Arctic sea-ice forecasts. The resulting adjusted forecasts are better calibrated, sharper, more realistic in their spatial structure, and retain more fine-scale spectral power than benchmark methods, including the standard cVAE.

Abstract: Seasonal climate predictions support planning and risk management by offering early information of the most likely-to-occur climate conditions in the coming months, and associated uncertainties. Ensemble forecasts enable this by simulating many plausible outcomes, allowing predictions to be expressed as usable probabilities. Large ensembles and high-resolution forecasts strengthen this guidance by better sampling uncertainty and capturing finer-scale processes but come with significant computational cost. Moreover, forecast ensembles drift and exhibit systematic biases and spatio-temporal errors that grow with lead time, requiring careful post-processing and calibration. A probabilistic post-processing framework based on conditional Variational Autoencoders (cVAEs) was developed at the Canadian Center for Climate Modeling and Analysis to generate large ensembles of bias adjusted seasonal predictions of Arctic sea ice. The generative model was designed to learn the observational distribution conditioned on the biased model prediction. This enables generation of arbitrarily large ensembles of well-calibrated, bias corrected forecasts with improved skill. Here, we extend this framework to address the loss of fine-scale energy and the characteristic blurriness in predictions, a known limitation of standard cVAEs. Specifically, we employ a generator in place of the Gaussian parametrized decoder in the cVAE and use Continuous Ranked Probability Score in the objective function instead of the Mean Square Error. We further use a higher resolution target dataset compared to the raw forecast. We show that the adjusted forecasts are better calibrated, more consistent with the observational distribution, and exhibit smaller errors than benchmark predictions, while also enhancing the resolution of the raw forecasts and improving sharpness and spectral power relative to the standard cVAE.

</details>


### [125] [When RL Suppresses Its Own Vocabulary: Recovering Reasoning Diversity in Puzzle-to-Math Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29190)
*Mayug Maniparambil,Arjun Karuvally,Terrence Sejnowski,Fergal Reid*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Puzzle-only post-training can transfer to hard math by building and composing reasoning primitives; adding a diversity incentive during RL preserves exploratory motifs and boosts transfer further.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL with verifiable rewards improves LLM reasoning, but it is unclear when cross-domain transfer happens and what mechanisms drive it.

Method: Train a 7B model with SFT and RL on constraint-satisfaction puzzles only; analyze transfer with a reasoning-primitive framework that uses a 9-class span classifier plus motif extraction; then modify RL with a novelty bonus based on perplexity under the reference model to encourage diverse correct rollouts.

Result: Puzzle SFT creates a reasoning-primitive vocabulary and gives +7 pp pass@32 on OlymMATH-Hard; vanilla GSPO adds another +6 pp by composing primitives into longer compute-verify chains but suppresses exploratory primitives like hypothesize and backtrack; the novelty bonus restores these primitives and adds +7 pp pass@32 over vanilla GSPO.

Conclusion: A puzzle-only post-training recipe can substantially improve cross-domain math reasoning in a 7B model, reaching 36.0% on OlymMATH-Hard without using math data during SFT or RL.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning using verifiable rewards (RLVR) improves LLM reasoning, but the conditions under which it transfers across domains -- and why it does so -- remain under-explored. We study cross-domain transfer in a 7B model whose SFT and RL post-training stages use only constraint-satisfaction puzzles, with no mathematics problems in the post-training data. To analyze how transfer emerges, we introduce a reasoning primitive-level framework that combines a 9-class span classifier with motif extraction, allowing us to segment chain-of-thought traces into primitive motifs and track their evolution across training stages and domains. We find that puzzle SFT induces a reasoning-primitive vocabulary, yielding a $+7$pp \texttt{pass@32} gain on OlymMATH-Hard. Vanilla GSPO then composes these primitives into longer compute-verify chains, adding a further $+6$pp. However, this RL stage also suppresses exploratory primitives such as \textit{hypothesize} and \textit{backtrack}. To address this, we introduce a novelty bonus that rewards diverse correct rollouts, using perplexity under the reference model as a signal. This restores recovery primitives during RL and adds a further $+7$pp \texttt{pass@32} relative to vanilla GSPO. Finally, the end-to-end recipe raises the hard-math capability ceiling from $16.0\%$ at the OLMo3-7B-Instruct-SFT base to $36.0\%$, without adding any mathematics problems during the SFT or RL stages.

</details>


### [126] [Inferring the Size of Large Language Models From Popular Text Memorization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29223)
*Ivica Nikolic*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This content has not passed the compliance test and has been hidden. / 此内容未通过合规检测，已隐藏。


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: This content has not passed the compliance test and has been hidden. / 此内容未通过合规检测，已隐藏。

Method: This content has not passed the compliance test and has been hidden. / 此内容未通过合规检测，已隐藏。

Result: This content has not passed the compliance test and has been hidden. / 此内容未通过合规检测，已隐藏。

Conclusion: This content has not passed the compliance test and has been hidden. / 此内容未通过合规检测，已隐藏。

Abstract: The parameter counts of the most widely used large language models (LLMs) are often withheld by their developers, leaving model size -- a primary reference point for interpreting capabilities and costs -- largely undisclosed. We propose a black-box method to infer conservative lower bounds on LLM size from generated text outputs alone, requiring nothing beyond the ability to submit text fragments and observe next-token predictions. Our approach is grounded in a key observation: popular, widely-circulated texts -- such as classical literature, religious texts, and foundational documents -- are present in virtually every large-scale pretraining corpus, and how accurately a model predicts the next word across text fragments of varying length is a reliable signal of how much it has memorized them, which in turn is fundamentally limited by its total parameter count. We aggregate this memorization signal across a diverse corpus of texts and fragment lengths into a single accuracy profile vector per model, and build two complementary inference methods on top of it: a pairwise statistical test that determines which of two models is larger, and a scaling-law estimator that extracts a one-dimensional latent index from these vectors via Principal Component Analysis (PCA) to map the aggregated signal to a parameter count. Validated on a broad set of open-weight models, both methods produce accurate and reliable lower bounds. When applied to popular closed-weight models, our framework recovers internal product hierarchies and reveals a clear divergence in industry scaling strategies: while some developers yield significantly higher bounds indicative of large generational parameter growth, others operate under strict parameter ceilings, demonstrating that hidden design choices can be systematically probed even under strict API limitations.

</details>


### [127] [Traditional machine learning vs. deep learning from dynamic graph representations of proteins' 3D folds in the task of protein structure classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29228)
*Aydin Wells,Francis A. Gatsi,Aaron Striegel,Tijana Milenković*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: For dynamic PSN-based protein structure classification, deep learning is usually not better than traditional machine learning, but it is much slower.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work showed that dynamic PSN features outperform static PSN, sequence-based, and direct 3D structural features for protein structure classification, but it remained unclear whether replacing traditional ML with deep learning would further improve performance.

Method: The paper compares traditional machine learning using engineered features from dynamic protein structure networks with deep learning models trained automatically on the same dynamic PSN representations across 72 datasets containing about 44,000 CATH- or SCOPe-labeled samples.

Result: Across 72 datasets, traditional ML and deep learning are tied or nearly tied in classification accuracy for the large majority of cases, while deep learning incurs substantially higher computational cost.

Conclusion: Deep learning on dynamic protein structure networks does not provide meaningful accuracy gains over traditional machine learning for protein structure classification, while being more than 10 times slower on average.

Abstract: Protein structure classification (PSC) uses supervised learning to predict a protein's CATH/SCOP(e) class from the protein's sequence or 3D structural feature(s). We already modeled 3D structures as (static) protein structure networks (PSNs), demonstrating the competitiveness of PSN-based features to sequence or direct (i.e. non-network) 3D structural features in the PSC task. More recently, we demonstrated the power of features extracted from dynamic PSNs over features extracted from static PSNs (and thus by transitivity over sequence and direct 3D structural features) in the same task. That dynamic PSN approach used traditional machine learning (ML), combining manual (pre-engineered) features with an off-the-shelf classifier. Here, we evaluate whether automatic deep learning (DL) from the dynamic PSNs yields improvements. Our evaluation on 72 datasets spanning ~44,000 CATH- or SCOPe-labeled dynamic PSNs reveals that in terms of PSC accuracy, traditional ML and DL are (close to) tied for a large majority of the datasets, while DL is on average 10+ times slower. We are the first to evaluate traditional ML vs. DL in the dynamic PSN-based PSC task.

</details>


### [128] [LoopFM: Learning frOm HistOrical RePresentations of Foundation Model for Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29280)
*Shali Jiang,Hua Zheng,Boyang Liu,Laming Chen,Kenny Lov,Chuanqi Xu,Lisang Ding,Qinghai Zhou,Can Cui,Xiaolong Liu,Xiaoyi Liu,Yasmine Badr,Xin Xu,Jiyan Yang,Ellie Dingqiao Wen,Gerard Jonathan Mugisha Akkerhuis,Chenxiao Guan,Rong Jin,Ruichao Qiu,Xian Chen,Shifu Xu,Zhehui Zhou,Ping Chen,Rui Yang,Haicheng Chen,Xiangge Meng,Song Zhou,Dharak Kharod,Shuyu Xu,Qiang Jin,Qiao Yang,Wankun Zhu,Qin Huang,Yuzhen Huang,Darren Liu,Parish Aggarwal,Hui Zhou,Erzhuo Wang,Shuo Chang,Xiaorui Gan,Wenlin Chen,Santanu Kolay,Huayu Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LoopFM improves on knowledge distillation by feeding historical FM embeddings into smaller downstream models, giving a much richer transfer signal and producing strong offline and online gains without requiring real-time FM serving.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard knowledge distillation passes only a single scalar prediction from a large foundation model to a smaller vertical model, which cannot capture the rich intermediate knowledge learned by the FM and leads to a diminishing transfer ratio as FM quality increases.

Method: The paper proposes LoopFM, which stores and structures foundation-model intermediate embeddings from past interactions as downstream input features, enabling vertical models to learn from rich FM representations without online FM inference or tight architectural coupling. It also provides a theoretical gain decomposition and transfer-ratio analysis, and studies complementarity with knowledge distillation.

Result: Across three public benchmarks, LoopFM achieves strong AUC improvements, including over 6% on TaobaoAd. In large-scale production systems, it roughly doubles the transfer ratio on top of KD and yields measurable conversion improvements: +0.5% in Y1H1 and +1.03% and +1.22% in two separate Y1H2 launches.

Conclusion: LoopFM opens a higher-bandwidth knowledge transfer channel by reusing historical FM intermediate representations as features for downstream vertical models, substantially improving transfer ratio and delivering consistent gains beyond standard KD in both public benchmarks and industrial deployment.

Abstract: Knowledge distillation (KD) transfers a single scalar prediction from a large foundation model (FM) to compact vertical models (VMs), suffering from diminishing transfer ratio -- the fraction of FM improvement captured by the VM -- as a single scalar cannot convey the rich intermediate knowledge that larger FMs learn. To address this bottleneck, we propose LoopFM (Learning frOm HistOrical ReP*resentations of FM), a framework that opens a high-bandwidth transfer channel by structuring FM intermediate embeddings as input features (e.g., user history sequence) for downstream VMs, without requiring real-time FM inference at serving and architectural coupling between FM and VM. We provide a theoretical framework for LoopFM with a gain decomposition and transfer-ratio analysis. On three public benchmarks, LoopFM demonstrates strong AUC improvements (e.g., 6\%+ on TaobaoAd) and complementary knowledge transfer capability with KD. On industrial-scale systems (billions of examples, trillion-parameter FMs), LoopFM approximately doubles the knowledge transfer ratio on top of KD, delivering a +0.5\% conversion improvement in Y1H1, and a +1.03\% and +1.22\% conversion improvement from two individual launches respectively in Y1H2.

</details>


### [129] [Deep Adaptive Dimension Reduction for Bayesian Inference in Inverse Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29373)
*Yueyang Wang,Xili Wang,Kejun Tang,Xiaoliang Wan,Tao Zhou,Chao Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A Variational Flow framework combines nonlinear dimension reduction, normalizing flows, adaptive prior updates, and an FNO surrogate to solve high-dimensional inverse problems more accurately and efficiently than several standard Bayesian baselines, especially in hard noisy/high-dimensional cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-dimensional PDE-governed inverse problems are difficult because posterior distributions are often complex and non-Gaussian, forward simulations are computationally expensive, and prior information may be misspecified. Existing methods struggle to jointly address all of these issues.

Method: The paper introduces a Variational Flow (VF) framework that integrates VAE-based nonlinear dimension reduction with dual normalizing flows for the latent prior and encoder, adds an iterative prior mean updating strategy, and couples these with an adaptively fine-tuned Fourier Neural Operator surrogate in a closed loop for posterior inference and surrogate refinement.

Result: Experiments on a 100-dimensional Rosenbrock problem and three PDE-governed inverse problems show that the method matches or outperforms MCMC, UKI, and SVGD across tested settings, with the largest gains under high-noise observations and high-dimensional parameter spaces.

Conclusion: The proposed Variational Flow-based adaptive Bayesian inference framework effectively solves high-dimensional PDE-governed inverse problems by combining nonlinear dimension reduction, flexible posterior approximation, adaptive prior updating, and iterative surrogate refinement, achieving strong performance especially in noisy and high-dimensional settings.

Abstract: Solving high-dimensional PDE-governed inverse problems is often challenging due to complex non-Gaussian posterior distributions, expensive forward model evaluations, and misspecified prior information. To address these issues, we propose a deep adaptive dimension-reduction Bayesian inference framework based on the Variational Flow (VF) model. Since standard normalizing flows are restricted by bijective mappings and cannot directly reduce dimensions, VF overcomes this limitation by integrating VAE-based nonlinear dimension reduction with dual normalizing flows for the latent prior and encoder. This design provides a strictly higher evidence lower bound than VAE and allows more flexible approximation of complex posterior distributions. We further introduce an iterative prior updating strategy that gradually moves the prior mean toward high-probability posterior regions, avoiding manual prior tuning. These components form a closed adaptive loop together with an adaptively fine-tuned Fourier Neural Operator (FNO) surrogate: VF generates posterior-concentrated samples to refine the surrogate, while the updated surrogate further improves posterior inference. Numerical experiments on a 100-dimensional Rosenbrock problem and three standard PDE-governed inverse problems show that our method delivers competitive or superior accuracy compared with MCMC, UKI, and SVGD baselines across all tested configurations, with the most pronounced advantages emerging in challenging scenarios such as high-noise observations and high-dimensional parameter spaces.

</details>


### [130] [The Good, the Bad, and the Ugly of Markov Boundary for Tabular Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29411)
*Shu Wan,Abhinav Gorantla,Huan Liu,K. Selçuk Candan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Markov boundary在理论上很适合做表格预测特征选择，而且oracle边界确实常常优于全特征；但“因果发现出边界，再拿边界训练模型”这条实际路线目前行不通。问题不在于边界本身没有价值，而在于现有发现方法与预测目标不对齐、代价太高、且预测上并不需要精确恢复唯一边界。 / The Markov boundary is theoretically attractive for tabular prediction, and the oracle boundary often does beat using all features; but the practical pipeline of discovering that boundary and training on it does not currently work well. The problem is not that the boundary lacks value, but that existing discovery methods are too costly, misaligned with prediction, and exact boundary recovery is unnecessary for predictive gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 理论上，Markov boundary是使其他特征在给定目标后都变得冗余的最小特征集，因此它看起来应当是表格预测中最理想的输入子集。但现实中现代回归器通常仍在全特征上训练。本文的动机是检验：Markov boundary是否真的能带来预测收益，以及现有因果发现方法能否把这种理论优势转化为实际可用的特征选择流程。 / The motivation is that, under graphical assumptions, the Markov boundary is the minimal feature set that renders all remaining features redundant with respect to the target, making it an apparently ideal input set for tabular prediction. Yet modern regressors are still trained on all features. The paper therefore asks whether the Markov boundary truly helps prediction and whether current causal discovery methods can turn that theoretical appeal into a practical feature-selection pipeline.

Method: 作者在SCM3K这个包含3450个合成结构因果模型任务的基准上进行系统实验，任务覆盖40到1000维特征、6类SCM家族，并使用6种回归器进行评估。研究比较了三种情形：使用全特征训练、使用oracle Markov boundary训练、以及通过因果发现估计边界后再训练。随后作者分析性能差异，并归因于优化目标、错误类型代价和可替代特征子集等因素。 / The paper conducts a systematic empirical study on SCM3K, a synthetic benchmark with 3,450 SCM tasks, 40-1000 features, six SCM families, and six regressors. It compares training on all features, training on the oracle Markov boundary, and training on boundaries recovered by causal discovery, then analyzes the resulting performance gaps in terms of objective mismatch, asymmetric error costs, and the existence of alternative predictive subsets.

Result: 实验结果表明，oracle边界相较全特征经常能显著提高预测性能，而且特征维度越大、冗余越强时提升越明显。然而，现有边界估计方法在最需要发挥作用的高维场景中往往耗尽计算预算；即使能够运行，恢复的边界通常也很少优于直接使用全特征。作者进一步识别出三类关键原因：结构恢复目标不等于预测最优，漏检与误检对预测的影响高度不对称，以及存在许多非精确边界但预测更优的特征集合。 / The experiments show that the oracle boundary often substantially outperforms the full feature set, with gains increasing in larger and sparser settings. However, current boundary estimators often exhaust the compute budget exactly in the high-dimensional regimes where the boundary would help most; even when they do run, the recovered boundaries rarely beat using all features. The paper identifies three main reasons: structural recovery is not the same as predictive optimization, false negatives and false positives have sharply asymmetric predictive effects, and many non-exact subsets can outperform the full set.

Conclusion: 在SCM3K基准上，Markov boundary作为oracle特征子集在很多情况下能显著提升表格预测，且在高维、稀疏特征空间中收益更大；但通过现有因果发现方法先恢复边界再训练预测器的自然流程通常无效，原因在于计算开销高、结构恢复目标与预测目标错位、边界误差的预测代价不对称，以及精确边界并不是唯一优于全特征集的特征子集。 / On SCM3K, the oracle Markov boundary often improves tabular prediction substantially, especially in larger and sparser feature spaces; however, the practical pipeline of recovering the boundary via causal discovery and then training on it usually fails because of high computational cost, misalignment between structural recovery and predictive objectives, asymmetric predictive costs of boundary errors, and the fact that the exact boundary is not the only feature subset that can outperform the full feature set.

Abstract: Under standard graphical assumptions, the Markov boundary of a target variable is the smallest set of features that renders every other feature redundant. Once the boundary is observed, the target is conditionally independent of the rest of the table. This is a tempting object for tabular prediction, since it names exactly the columns a model should need. Yet modern regressors are still trained on the full feature set. We ask whether the Markov boundary is genuinely useful for prediction on SCM3K, a 3,450-task synthetic SCM benchmark with feature counts from 40 to 1000 and six SCM families, evaluated with six regressors. The answer is more nuanced than the theory suggests. Restricting a regressor to the oracle boundary often improves prediction substantially, and the improvement grows as the feature space becomes larger and sparser. But the natural pipeline of recovering the boundary with causal discovery and training on the recovered mask does not deliver. Existing estimators exhaust the compute budget before reaching the regime where the boundary helps most, and even where they run they rarely beat the full feature set. We trace this to three causes. Discovery optimizes structural recovery rather than prediction. False negatives and false positives carry sharply asymmetric predictive cost. The exact boundary is only one of many feature sets that beat all features. We then develop what these facts imply for prediction-aligned feature selection and for tabular models that learn to use causal structure.

</details>


### [131] [SCOPE: A Lightweight-training LLM Framework for Air Traffic Control Readback Monitoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29543)
*Qihan Deng,Minghua Zhang,Yang Yang,Zhenyu Gao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SCOPE is a lightweight frozen-LLM approach for ATC readback monitoring that uses open-set classification plus in-context examples, achieving high accuracy, low latency, and interpretable decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ATC pilot readback is critical for preventing miscommunication, yet readback anomalies are involved in many aviation incidents. Existing rule-based and traditional machine learning methods generalize poorly to diverse and evolving phraseology, while prior LLM approaches remain too costly or difficult to deploy.

Method: The paper proposes SCOPE, a lightweight-training framework that combines a frozen large language model, a plug-in open-set classifier, and a carefully designed in-context learning strategy to detect and correct anomalous ATC pilot readbacks.

Result: On a semi-synthetic communication dataset in a few-shot setting, SCOPE achieves 91.05% accuracy for open-set detection and 96.63% correction accuracy for anomalous readbacks, outperforming strong baselines while also providing explanations.

Conclusion: SCOPE demonstrates that a frozen-LLM framework augmented with an open-set plug-in classifier and in-context examples can provide practical, interpretable, and controllable ATC readback monitoring with strong accuracy and low latency.

Abstract: Pilot readback of Air Traffic Control (ATC) voice instructions is a primary safeguard against miscommunication in air transportation. However, readback anomalies remain implicated in approximately 80% of aviation incidents. This vulnerability is further exacerbated by rising traffic volume and elevated cognitive workload, thereby motivating automated readback monitoring by machine. Traditional rule-based and machine learning approaches struggle to generalize across the highly variable and evolving phraseology of air traffic controller-pilot communications. While Large Language Models (LLMs) have opened a new avenue through their strong reasoning and generalization capabilities, existing approaches still face deployment and computational barriers in practice. In this work, we propose Semantic reasoning for Communication via Open-set Plug-in with Examples (SCOPE), a novel lightweight-training LLM framework that advances both the efficiency and accuracy of machine-based ATC readback monitoring. The core idea is to couple a plug-in open-set classifier with a carefully designed in-context learning mechanism on top of a frozen LLM. Extensive experiments on the semi-synthetic communication dataset show that SCOPE attains superior accuracy while delivering the low-latency response required for operational environments. Under a few-shot setting, SCOPE achieves 91.05% accuracy in open-set detection and corrects 96.63% of anomalous readbacks, thereby outperforming the strongest available baselines while providing explanations for its decisions. These findings demonstrate the potential of our framework as a practical pathway toward interpretable and controllable ATC readback monitoring.

</details>


### [132] [Singularity-aware Optimization via Randomized Geometric Probing: Towards Stable Non-smooth Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29547)
*Ruoran Xu,Borong She,Xiaobo Jin,Qiufeng Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Summary generation failed

Abstract: Deep learning optimization relies heavily on the assumption of smooth loss landscapes, a condition systematically violated by modern architectures due to non-smooth components such as ReLU activations and quantization operators. In such non-smooth regimes, adaptive optimizers such as Adam suffer from gradient chattering, violent oscillations caused by conflicting signals within the Clarke subdifferential, leading to poor convergence and suboptimal generalization. To address this, we introduce Singularity-aware Adam (S-Adam), a novel optimizer that stabilizes training by dynamically modulating step sizes based on local geometric instability. Our key contribution is the Local Geometric Instability (LGI) metric, a computationally efficient estimator of the Clarke subdifferential diameter derived from the variance of randomized directional derivatives. S-Adam incorporates an adaptive damping mechanism exp(-$λ$$ρ$) that decelerates updates in high-instability regions while preserving fast convergence in smooth basins. We provide a rigorous convergence analysis using differential inclusions, proving that S-Adam converges almost surely to ($δ$,$ε$)-Clarke stationary points at the optimal O(1/$\sqrt(T)$) rate. Empirical evaluations on Quantization-Aware Training (QAT) and high-noise small-batch learning demonstrate that S-Adam consistently outperforms AdamW and Prox-SGD, achieving accuracy gains of up to 6 percent on CIFAR-100 and 3 percent on TinyImageNet while effectively mitigating gradient oscillations.

</details>


### [133] [Cluster-Level Attention-Guided Parallel Decoding for Masked Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29607)
*Heqiang Qi,Wei Huang,Mingyuan Bai,Xiangming Meng*

Abstract: Masked diffusion language models (MDLMs) enable parallel decoding by predicting all masked positions at each denoising step, yet existing training-free samplers usually decide which positions to commit at token-level granularity. We revisit this granularity and observe that reliable predictions often emerge as contiguous high-confidence spans, suggesting that the unit of parallel commitment can be larger than a single token. We first group adjacent high-confidence candidates into confidence-induced clusters (CICs) as span-level update units. We then use self-attention maps from the same forward pass to estimate inter-cluster dependencies, enabling conflict-aware selection of mutually compatible CICs for parallel commitment. This yields CLAD (Cluster-Level Attention-Guided Decoding), a training-free cluster-level decoder for MDLMs. Experiments on LLaDA and Dream model families across four reasoning and code-generation benchmarks show that CLAD achieves 1.77x--8.47x speedups over Vanilla decoding while maintaining broadly comparable task accuracy in most settings.

</details>

Abstract: Coupled-cluster (CC) theory is often considered the gold standard of quantum chemistry, but its high computational cost limits routine access to accurate energies, forces and response properties. While the right-hand $T$-amplitudes determine the correlated wavefunction, many practically important observables additionally require the left-hand $Λ$-amplitudes. We introduce MōLe-$Λ$, an extension of Molecular Orbital Learning (MōLe) that predicts the full ground-state coupled-cluster singles and doubles (CCSD) response state by jointly learning right-hand amplitudes $(T_1,T_2)$ and left-hand amplitudes $(Λ_1,Λ_2)$ from localized Hartree--Fock molecular orbitals. Architecturally, MōLe-$Λ$ extends MōLe with $Λ_1$ and $Λ_2$ readouts that mirror the symmetry constraints of the $T_1$ and $T_2$ heads, while preserving the original equivariant orbital encoder, odd sign-equivariant decoding, locality and size-extensivity. The resulting model yields accurate CC-quality energies and forces, while simultaneously recovering dipoles, quadrupoles, polarizabilities, the electron density, and 2-electron observables such as the pair density. We show that MōLe-$Λ$ further extends the speed advantage of MōLe over full CCSD while substantially expanding the accessible properties, providing a route to wavefunction-level surrogate models for correlated quantum chemistry.

</details>

Abstract: This paper presents the Tensor Product Network (TPNet), a novel neural architecture for efficient and accurate function approximation and PDE solving. The core of the proposal involves constructing the solution explicitly as a linear combination of basis functions integrated into the network, with coefficients determined by a direct least-squares solve, thereby bypassing traditional gradient-based training. The key methodological contribution include: (1) an efficient tensor-product scheme that generates multi-dimensional basis functions from combinations of two sets of subnetwork outputs, significantly reducing model complexity and parameter count while maintaining expressivity; (2) a block time-marching strategy to improve computational efficiency in long-time simulations; and (3) a linear reformulation strategy for handling nonlinear PDEs by treating known nonlinear terms as sources. TPNet achieves superior accuracy and shorter training times than conventional neural network solvers. This performance gain stems from its structured design and deterministic least-squares fitting, which contrast with the iterative, often computationally intensive optimization required by mainstream methods like Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs).

</details>

Abstract: We introduce scheming honeypot evaluations, a framework for testing whether models will pursue instrumental goals if given the opportunity. Our scheming honeypot evaluations take the form of coding tasks in Google's alignment research codebases. In a real internal deployment setting, Gemini models do not demonstrate unprompted scheming. If prompts explicitly encourage agency (situational awareness or goal-directedness) and/or give the model a hidden goal, models sometimes scheme or attempt sabotage. Validating the realism of our setting, models show low rates of evaluation awareness, usually due to agency prompts rather than the environments.

</details>
