Abstract: High-density electroencephalography (HD-EEG) enables fine-grained measurement of cortical activity but requires expensive hardware and lengthy setup times, limiting its clinical and research accessibility. We propose EMAG (EEG Mixture of Anisotropic Gaussians), a differentiable framework that reconstructs HD-EEG signals from a sparse subset of low-density (LD) electrodes by representing brain electrical sources as a mixture of anisotropic 4D space-time Gaussians. EMAG places a mixture of multiple Gaussians at each point of a spherical brain grid, each parameterized by a full 4 x 4 precision matrix, enabling anisotropic spatial spreads and explicit coupling between spatial and temporal dimensions. The forward model renders scalp EEG via differentiable Gaussian field contributions at electrode locations, enabling end-to-end training without explicit source localization supervision. We evaluate EMAG on three public EEG benchmarks (Localize-MI, SEED, and SEED-IV) at super-resolution factors of 2x through 8/16x. EMAG outperforms the current state-of-the-art EEG super-resolution method at most super-resolution factors on three standard benchmarks (Localize-MI, SEED, SEED-IV). The explicit Gaussian parameterization further enables direct visualization and interpretability of learned brain source configurations, potentially opening avenues for clinical and neuroscientific applications, such as source localization or biomarker discovery.

</details>


### [138] [MMTM: Tri-Modal Topic Modeling for Long-Form Video via Similarity-Gated Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29765)
*Ali Abusaleh,Bhuvanesh Verma,Alexander Mehler*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MMTM is a modular tri-modal video topic discovery pipeline that fuses speech, audio, and visual embeddings with BERTopic, producing markedly cleaner, more stable, and better-clustered topics across multilingual news video, with some corpus-dependent limits in lexical coherence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Topic discovery in long-form video is difficult when relying on a single modality because speech, sound, and visuals each capture only part of the semantic structure. The work aims to improve robustness, coherence, and temporal stability of discovered topics in multilingual broadcast news by integrating multiple modalities in a modular way.

Method: The paper proposes MMTM, a modular pipeline for long-form video topic discovery. It combines automatic speech recognition, audio embeddings, visual embeddings, and BERTopic clustering, then applies a deterministic similarity-gated fusion strategy to jointly model the three modalities.

Result: On German Tagesschau and English NBC news, joint tri-modal modeling reduces noise from 0.27 to 0.06, lowers transition rate from 0.70 to 0.21, increases normalized entropy from 0.84 to 0.92, and improves Calinski-Harabasz cluster validity by 5-12X. NPMI lexical coherence improves from 0.77 to 0.86 on German but does not transfer consistently to the shorter NBC corpus. The authors also release code and a 54-hour human-validated multimodal topic corpus.

Conclusion: MMTM demonstrates that deterministic tri-modal fusion of speech, audio, and visual signals yields more coherent, temporally stable, and better-separated topics for long-form video topic discovery across German and English broadcast news, though lexical coherence gains are corpus-dependent.

Abstract: We introduce MMTM, a modular pipeline for topic discovery in long-form video that integrates speech recognition, audio and visual embeddings, and BERTopic clustering through a deterministic similarity-gated fusion. Evaluated cross-lingually on German (Tagesschau) and English (NBC) broadcast news, joint tri-modal modeling substantially improves topic quality: noise drops from 0.27 to 0.06, transition rate from 0.70 to 0.21, and normalized entropy rises from 0.84 to 0.92, indicating more coherent and temporally stable topics. Cluster validity (Calinski-Harabasz) improves by 5-12X across embedding spaces. Lexical coherence (NPMI) rises from 0.77 to 0.86 on German but is corpus-dependent and does not transfer to the shorter NBC broadcasts. We release the pipeline code and a human-validated 54-hour multimodal video topic corpus with dual-annotator visual evaluation and LLM-assisted labeling.

</details>


### [139] [Hista and Numca: Estimate State Value Effectively for LLM Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29782)
*Zizhe Chen,Jiqian Dong,Yizhou Tian,Garry Yang,Yongqiang Chen,Zhitang Chen,James Cheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper shows that value critics in LLM RL are often weak, introduces a benchmark to measure the problem, and proposes Numca and Hista to estimate state values better and improve RL post-training efficiently.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: State value estimation is central to stable reinforcement learning, but in LLM post-training it is underexplored and existing critics in methods like PPO may collapse to coarse group-average estimates.

Method: The paper introduces SVEB, a benchmark for evaluating state value estimation in LLM RL, and proposes two methods: Numca, which uses numerical spans as intermediate gradable milestones, and Hista, which estimates value by using LLM hidden-state representations to compute weighted averages over disjoint rollouts and returns.

Result: Experiments show standard critics perform poorly on state estimation under SVEB, while Numca and Hista produce more accurate value estimates and improve downstream RL training performance without significant computational overhead.

Conclusion: Numca and Hista improve state value estimation for LLM post-training RL, leading to more stable and effective training across RL algorithms and model scales with little extra compute.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) refines large language models (LLMs) by directly optimizing model behavior through reward signals. While accurate state value estimation is critical for stable training in classical RL, it remains an underexplored challenge in LLM post-training. In this work, we introduce the State Value Estimation Benchmark (SVEB) to assess state estimation within existing RL frameworks and show that critics in standard approaches like PPO collapse to a coarse group-average baseline. To address this, we propose two techniques: Numca, which leverages numerical spans as gradable milestones for state value estimation, and Hista, a framework that uses LLM's hidden states as representation to weighted average disjoint rollouts and their return. Extensive experiments demonstrate that both methods yield more accurate state value estimates and enhance training performance across different RL algorithms and model sizes without incurring significant computational overhead.

</details>


### [140] [Gated Graph Attention Networks with Learnable Temperature](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29803)
*Zhongtian Ma,Hao Wu,Yexin Zhang,Qiaosheng Zhang,Zhen Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Add gates to filter bad feature dimensions and learn the attention temperature to control sharpness; both make graph attention networks more robust and usually more accurate, especially when features are noisy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard graph attention networks can weight neighbors adaptively, but they do not explicitly filter unreliable feature dimensions and they use fixed attention sharpness, which can limit robustness under noisy or weakly discriminative features.

Method: It extends standard graph attention with two components: gated graph attention to suppress unreliable feature/message dimensions, and a learnable temperature parameter to adapt the sharpness of attention coefficient distributions.

Result: Experiments on homogeneous, heterogeneous, and heterophilic benchmarks show consistent gains over baseline graph attention backbones, and controlled noise experiments plus theory support why gating and temperature help under different noise conditions.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that adding gating and learnable temperature to graph attention mechanisms consistently improves robustness and performance across multiple graph benchmarks, especially under noisy or partially unreliable features.

Abstract: Graph attention networks learn neighbor importance through data-dependent coefficients, but standard layers lack explicit control over unreliable feature dimensions and use fixed sharpness of attention coefficient distributions. This paper proposes gated graph attention and learnable temperature for common graph attention mechanisms. Gated graph attention filters feature or message responses to reduce the influence of unreliable dimensions, while learnable temperature dynamically adjusts the sharpness of the attention coefficient distribution. Experiments on homogeneous and heterophilic heterogeneous benchmarks show that the proposed variants consistently improve the corresponding graph attention backbones, and controlled noise studies further verify their behavior under feature perturbations. Theoretical analysis explains these results by showing that gating improves robustness when only part of the feature coordinates are reliable, while temperature is beneficial when global noise weakens the discriminability of node features.

</details>


### [141] [The Interplay Between Interpolation and Aggregation in Regression: Optimal Sample Complexity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29819)
*Mikael Møller Høgsgaard,Kasper Green Larsen,Liang-Yu Zou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Aggregation can make regression learnable in ways proper learning cannot: γ-graph dimension is the key complexity measure, median-of-three is optimal for a natural class of rules, and some classes need either infinitely many hypotheses or non-interpolating aggregation to be learnable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work aims to understand how interpolation and aggregation interact in regression, especially whether aggregating multiple interpolating hypotheses can overcome limitations of proper learning and what structural measure governs learnability.

Method: The authors give a theoretical analysis of regression learnability under interpolation and aggregation. They characterize broad natural aggregation procedures via the γ-graph dimension, analyze a simple 3-hypothesis median aggregator, and compare finite interpolating aggregation against infinite or non-interpolating aggregation rules.

Result: They prove that γ-graph dimension characterizes learnability for a wide class of aggregation procedures. They also show that median-of-three interpolation is optimal among these procedures and strictly more powerful than proper learning. Finally, they establish lower bounds showing that some hypothesis classes cannot be learned by any finite interpolating aggregation, even trivially, unless one allows infinitely many hypotheses or non-interpolating outputs.

Conclusion: The paper shows that aggregation can substantially extend learnability in regression beyond proper learning. It identifies the γ-graph dimension as the key characterization for a broad family of aggregation rules, proves that median aggregation of three interpolating hypotheses is optimal within that family, and shows that some classes require either infinitely many hypotheses or non-interpolating aggregation to be learnable.

Abstract: This work investigates theoretically the interplay between interpolation and aggregation in regression. We establish that the $γ$-graph dimension characterizes learnability for a broad class of natural aggregation procedures. Furthermore, we prove that an extremely simple aggregation procedure, combining three interpolating hypotheses via the median, is optimal among all these aggregation procedures, and is strictly more powerful than proper learning. Finally, we show that some hypothesis classes are learnable only by aggregating infinitely many hypotheses or by using non-interpolating aggregation rules (which may predict outside the range of their inputs), and any finite interpolating aggregation fails to achieve even trivial performance.

</details>


### [142] [When Do Graph Foundation Models Transfer? A Data-Centric Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29828)
*Jiajun Zhu,Ying Chen,Peihao Wang,Yixuan He,Pan Li,Aditya Akella,Zhangyang Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper explains when a fixed graph foundation model transfers well: transfer degradation comes from sampling noise plus true structural mismatch between source and target graph domains, and stable positional encodings are essential. The theory gives practical guidance for selecting or curating transfer data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Graph foundation models are intended to share one backbone across diverse graph domains, but transfer quality is inconsistent and can even become negative. Prior work mainly adjusts architecture or adaptation strategy; this paper instead asks a data-centric question: which domain properties themselves determine whether a fixed model’s outputs change substantially across domains?

Method: The paper uses a graphon-based continuous limit for dense graphs to analyze transfer of a fixed Lipschitz representation backbone across domains. It derives explicit bounds for cross-domain output shift under both set-based and message-passing tokenizations, and proves stability guarantees for spectral positional encodings, comparing eigenvector-based and subspace-based PEs. The theory is then validated with synthetic and real-graph experiments.

Result: The authors derive a theoretical decomposition of cross-domain output shift into finite-sample approximation terms and an intrinsic structural domain discrepancy. They establish positional encoding stability results, showing contrasting stability behavior between eigenvector-based and subspace-based spectral PEs. Experiments on synthetic and real graphs support the theory and show how the decomposition can guide data curation for better transfer.

Conclusion: For graph foundation model transfer, the output shift between graph domains is governed by a clean decomposition: finite-sample graph-specific approximation error plus an intrinsic, relabeling-invariant domain discrepancy that reflects structural mismatch. Positional encoding stability is a critical factor, with spectral PE stability depending on whether eigenvector-based or subspace-based constructions are used.

Abstract: Graph foundation models (GFMs) aim to reuse a single backbone across diverse graph domains, yet their transfer is often uneven and can exhibit negative transfer. While most prior work improves transfer through architectural or adaptation choices, we ask a data-centric question: which properties of two graph domains determine how much a fixed representation model changes its outputs? Using a graphon-based continuous limit for dense graphs, we show that for both set-based and message-passing tokenizations, any Lipschitz backbone admits an explicit decomposition of cross-domain output shift into (i) graph-specific finite-sample approximation terms and (ii) an intrinsic, relabeling-invariant domain discrepancy capturing structural mismatch. A key ingredient is positional-encoding (PE) stability: we establish stability guarantees for spectral PEs and highlight contrasting behaviors of eigenvector- versus subspace-based PEs. Experiments on synthetic and real graphs validate the theory and translate the decomposition into guidance for data curation in GFM transfer.

</details>


### [143] [Open World Autoencoding Drift Detection with Novel Class Recognition in Tabular Non-stationary Data Streams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29834)
*Joanna Komorniczak*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An unsupervised mirrored-autoencoder approach detects concept drift via reconstruction error and identifies novel classes via density estimation, adapting incrementally over time and matching state-of-the-art results on synthetic streaming data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is that data stream learning faces two major challenges at once: concept drift in known classes and the appearance of previously unseen classes. Existing recognition methods need unsupervised, continuously adaptive mechanisms that can address both problems reliably in evolving streams.

Method: The method uses mirrored autoencoders. One branch detects concept drift by monitoring reconstruction errors for known-class distribution shifts, while the other recognizes novel class samples through density estimation over a proxy representation. The mirrored design enables separate incremental adaptation for drift detection and novelty recognition.

Result: Experiments on diverse synthetic tabular data streams containing both drifts and novelties show that the proposed approach performs competitively with current state-of-the-art unsupervised drift detectors and novelty classifiers.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that a mirrored-autoencoder framework can jointly handle unsupervised concept drift detection and novel class recognition in evolving data streams, achieving competitive performance against state-of-the-art methods on synthetic tabular streams.

Abstract: Data stream processing has become a landmark in modern machine learning applications, with concept drifts and novel class appearances posing the primary challenges faced by sophisticated recognition methods. This work proposes an unsupervised concept drift detection method that identifies shifts in known class distributions based on the reconstruction errors of an autoencoder, while also enabling the recognition of novel class samples through density estimation of a proxy representation of samples. Using mirrored autoencoders allows for independent incremental adaptation to changing problem distributions for the two considered tasks, resulting in continuous adjustment to evolving concepts and reliable recognition of unknown samples. Conducted experiments used a diverse set of synthetic tabular data streams, where both concept drifts and the emergence of novelties were observed. The results show that the proposed approach is competitive with current state-of-the-art unsupervised drift detectors and novelty classifiers.

</details>


### [144] [CB-SLICE: Concept-Based Interpretable Error Slice Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29836)
*Yael Konforti,Mateo Espinosa Zarlenga,Elaf Almahmoud,Mateja Jamnik*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CB-SLICE finds groups of examples that fail for the same concept-level reasons in Concept Bottleneck Models, giving more accurate and interpretable error-slice explanations than previous methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing error slice discovery methods often produce explanations that are weakly connected to the model's actual inference process, so their identified error sources can be approximate or inaccurate. The authors aim to provide slice explanations that are both fine-grained and causally closer to the model's failures.

Method: The paper builds on Concept Bottleneck Models, where predictions depend on human-interpretable concepts. It groups samples that share similar concept mispredictions and then extracts the key concepts most responsible for each group's failure mode, forming a concept-based slice discovery method called CB-SLICE.

Result: Across multiple benchmarks, CB-SLICE is shown to better uncover known biases than state-of-the-art methods while also producing richer and more faithful explanations of model errors.

Conclusion: CB-SLICE uses concept prediction failures in Concept Bottleneck Models to identify error slices and their root causes more faithfully than prior slice discovery methods, and it outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across several benchmarks.

Abstract: Despite strong average-case performance, deep learning models often exhibit systematic errors on specific population groups, known as error slices. Identifying these groups and the root causes of their failures is critical for model debugging and bias mitigation. However, existing error Slice Discovery Methods (SDMs) typically generate explanations disconnected from the model's inference process, thus only approximating the underlying error source and may be inaccurate.
  We address this limitation by leveraging Concept Bottleneck Models (CBMs), whose predictions are directly dependent on human-understandable semantic concepts. Since downstream task failures in CBMs commonly arise from concept mispredictions, concept representations provide a strong candidate for error slice identification, offering fine-grained explanations directly linked to the error source. Building on this insight, we introduce CB-SLICE, a concept-based SDM that groups samples with shared concept prediction failures and identifies the keyword concepts most responsible for each slice's failure mode. Across multiple benchmarks, we show that CB-SLICE outperforms state-of-the-art methods in uncovering well-known biases while providing richer and more faithful explanations of model errors.

</details>


### [145] [Beyond MSE: Improving Precipitation Nowcasting with Multi-Quantile Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30122)
*Gijs van Nieuwkoop,Siamak Mehrkanoon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 本文表明：把降水临近预报从单点回归改成多分位数回归，是一种低改造成本但有效的训练策略，既能提升主预测精度，又能更好支持强降水风险评估。 / The paper shows that switching precipitation nowcasting from point estimation to multi-quantile regression is a low-overhead but effective training change that improves core forecast accuracy and better supports heavy-rain risk assessment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 传统点对点损失如 MSE/MAE 往往导致预报过度平滑，尤其难以刻画强降水；作者希望检验，多分位数回归是否能作为一种简单替代方案，同时提升整体预测质量与极端降水表征能力。 / Standard pointwise losses such as MSE and MAE often produce overly smooth forecasts and weak heavy-rain representation; the authors investigate whether multi-quantile regression can serve as a simple alternative that improves both overall accuracy and extreme-precipitation characterization.

Method: 以 SmaAt-UNet 为核心模型，在荷兰雷达降水临近预报任务上，对比三种训练目标：MSE、MAE 和多分位数 pinball loss；评估其对中心确定性预测与强降水上分位输出的影响。 / The paper uses SmaAt-UNet as the base architecture and compares three training objectives on Dutch radar precipitation nowcasting: MSE, MAE, and multi-quantile pinball loss, evaluating both central deterministic forecasts and upper-quantile outputs for heavy precipitation.

Result: 多分位数训练将测试集上的中心确定性预测 MSE 相比 MSE 训练降低了 8.6%，并生成了可用于强降水风险敏感预测的上分位输出。 / Multi-quantile training reduces test-set MSE of the central deterministic forecast by 8.6% relative to standard MSE training, while also producing upper-quantile outputs useful for risk-sensitive heavy-rain prediction.

Conclusion: 将降水临近预报训练重构为多分位数回归，可以在不更换架构、也不引入生成式采样的前提下，提升确定性预报表现，并额外提供对强降水更有价值的风险敏感上分位预测。 / Reformulating precipitation nowcasting training as multi-quantile regression improves deterministic forecast quality without changing the architecture or adding generative sampling, while also yielding useful upper-quantile predictions for heavy-rain risk awareness.

Abstract: Deep-learning precipitation nowcasting models are often optimized using pointwise losses such as mean squared error or mean absolute error, which can lead to overly smooth forecasts and poor representation of heavy rainfall. This study investigates whether the predictive performance of an established deterministic nowcasting architecture can be improved by reformulating training as a multi-quantile regression problem. Using SmaAt-UNet as a core model, we compare MSE, MAE, and multi-quantile pinball-loss training on radar precipitation nowcasting over the Netherlands. The results show that multi-quantile training improves the central deterministic forecast, decreasing test-set MSE by 8.6\% compared to a model trained using MSE, while also producing upper-quantile outputs that are useful for risk-sensitive prediction of heavy precipitation. These findings suggest that quantile regression provides a simple alternative to standard pointwise losses without requiring a new architecture or generative sampling procedure. The implementation of our models and training setup is available on \href{https://github.com/gijsvn/Multi-Quantile-Precipitation-Nowcasting}{GitHub}.

</details>


### [146] [Learning to Extrapolate to New Tasks: A Relational Approach to Task Extrapolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30132)
*Adam Ousherovitch,Yixin Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 中文：这篇论文认为“外推本质上是任务之间关系的迁移”。RTE 通过学习任务如何相互变换，而不是只拟合单个任务，从而更好地解决未见任务外推。 English: The core idea is that extrapolation is relational: by learning how tasks transform into one another rather than only fitting individual tasks, RTE achieves stronger extrapolation to unseen tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 中文：现代学习系统虽然擅长插值，但在训练分布支持之外的未见任务上泛化很差，尤其是参数超范围、更深组合长度和新组合结构等简单外推场景中依然失败，因此需要一种面向任务外推的新机制。 English: Modern learning systems are strong at interpolation but weak at generalizing to unseen tasks outside the training distribution, even in simple extrapolation settings such as out-of-range parameters, longer compositions, and unseen recombinations, motivating a mechanism specifically for task extrapolation.

Method: 中文：论文提出 Relational Task Extrapolator（RTE）。它将目标任务分解为一个已知锚点任务和一个连接锚点与目标的变换，然后学习一个关系算子，将“锚点任务 + 变换”映射为目标任务的预测；并将该框架应用于函数预测与序列预测/基础模型微调。 English: The paper proposes the Relational Task Extrapolator (RTE), which decomposes a target task into a known anchor task plus a transformation from anchor to target, and learns a relational operator that maps the anchor-transformation pair to predictions for the target task; it is instantiated for function prediction and sequence prediction/foundation-model fine-tuning.

Result: 中文：在参数外推、长度外推、组合外推以及序列预测等多种实验中，RTE 都显著优于现有方法，表现出更强的未见任务外推能力。 English: Across parameter, length, and compositional extrapolation in function prediction, as well as sequence prediction settings, RTE substantially outperforms existing approaches on extrapolation to novel unseen tasks.

Conclusion: 中文：RTE 通过显式建模任务之间的关系变换，能够把训练中学到的任务间变换迁移到未见任务上，从而显著提升系统性外推能力。 English: RTE shows that explicitly modeling transformations between tasks enables systematic extrapolation to unseen tasks and substantially improves generalization beyond the training support.

Abstract: Modern learning systems excel at interpolation but struggle to generalize to unseen tasks outside the training distribution's support. This failure occurs even in simple settings, such as handling task parameters beyond the training range, and persists despite advances in foundation models. To this end, we develop the Relational Task Extrapolator (RTE), an algorithm designed to enable systematic extrapolation to novel tasks. The key observation is that extrapolation is inherently relational: extrapolating to unseen tasks requires learning how tasks transform into one another. If a model learns the transformation between tasks A and B during training, it can apply that same transformation to relate known tasks to unseen ones at test time. RTE operationalizes this idea by decomposing each target task into a known anchor task and a transformation linking the anchor and target. It then learns a relational operator, mapping an anchor-transformation pair to predictions for the target task. We instantiate RTE across multiple task extrapolation regimes in function prediction, e.g. where target tasks use out-of-range parameters (parameter extrapolation), have greater compositional depth (length extrapolation), and/or recombine function primitives in unseen ways (compositional extrapolation). We further extend RTE to sequence prediction, integrating it into fine-tuning algorithms for foundation models. Across empirical studies, we find that RTE substantially outperforms existing approaches on extrapolation to novel, unseen tasks.

</details>


### [147] [Overcoming Forgetting in LLM Fine-Tuning with Evolution Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30148)
*Kajetan Schweighofer,Conor F. Hayes,Roberto Dailey,Risto Miikkulainen,Xin Qiu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TL;DR：这篇论文认为 ES 微调中的“遗忘”通常不是永久性的，而是训练过程中的性能漂移；作者分析其根源是参数空间弱约束方向上的随机游走，并提出 AWD 把参数拉回初始模型附近，从而用较低成本同时保住旧任务和新任务表现。 / TL;DR: The paper argues that ES 'forgetting' is usually temporary performance drift, not permanent loss; it traces the cause to random walks in weakly constrained parameter directions and introduces AWD to keep weights near the initial model, preserving both old-task and new-task performance efficiently.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 动机是：虽然 ES 因简单、可扩展、仅依赖推理而成为 LLM 微调的有竞争力替代方案，但已有工作认为它在新任务训练时会遗忘旧任务，这限制了其在持续学习中的应用，因此需要重新理解该现象并寻找低成本缓解方法。 / The motivation is that ES has become an attractive alternative to RL for LLM fine-tuning due to simplicity, scalability, and inference-only training, but reports of prior-task forgetting limit its use in continual learning, motivating a re-examination of the phenomenon and a low-cost mitigation strategy.

Method: 方法上，论文首先比较并追踪 ES 与 RL 微调过程中旧任务性能的动态变化，分析漂移与训练动力学、尤其是参数空间中弱约束方向上的随机游走之间的关系；随后提出 AWD，通过将参数正则化到初始模型权重附近来约束优化。 / Methodologically, the paper tracks prior-task performance during ES and RL fine-tuning, analyzes drift through training dynamics—especially random walks along weakly constrained directions in weight space—and then proposes AWD, a regularization method that anchors parameters toward the initial model weights.

Result: 结果表明：旧任务性能下降常会在训练过程中恢复，因此更应被视为性能漂移；这一现象并非 ES 独有，RL 微调中也会出现；漂移与 ES 的训练动力学密切相关；AWD 能在几乎不损害目标任务性能的情况下显著稳定旧任务表现，其效果可媲美更大的 ES 种群规模但计算成本更低。 / The results show that drops in prior-task performance often recover during training, so the issue is better viewed as performance drift; it is not unique to ES and also appears in RL fine-tuning; the drift is linked to ES training dynamics; and AWD substantially stabilizes prior-task performance with little loss on the target task, matching benefits of larger ES populations at much lower compute cost.

Conclusion: 论文结论是：ES 在 LLM 持续微调中出现的“旧任务遗忘”大多并非不可逆遗忘，而是可避免的性能漂移；通过对初始参数进行约束的 Anchored Weight Decay（AWD），可以以较低计算成本稳定旧任务表现，同时保持新任务性能，使 ES 成为有前景的持续学习方案。 / The paper concludes that prior-task 'forgetting' in ES-based LLM fine-tuning is mostly avoidable performance drift rather than irreversible forgetting; Anchored Weight Decay (AWD) can stabilize prior-task performance at low cost while preserving target-task gains, making ES promising for continual learning.

Abstract: Evolution Strategies (ES) has recently emerged as a competitive alternative to reinforcement learning (RL) for large language model (LLM) fine-tuning, offering advantages through simplicity, scalability, and inference-only training. However, recent work suggests that ES fine-tuning on new tasks may induce forgetting of prior tasks. First, this paper shows that prior task forgetting (1) is better characterized as performance drift rather than irreversible forgetting, with prior-task performance often recovering during ES training; and (2) is not a specific failure mode of ES, but can also arise for fine-tuning with RL methods. Second, it analyzes when and why such drift arises, highlighting its dependence on ES training dynamics, particularly random walk behavior in weakly constrained directions of the weight space. Third, based on these insights, it introduces Anchored Weight Decay (AWD) as a parameter-space regularization technique that constrains optimization toward the initial model parameters. AWD effectively stabilizes prior-task performance while preserving target-task performance, achieving benefits comparable to large ES population sizes at much lower computational cost. Thus, contrary to previous beliefs, the paper shows that prior-task forgetting under ES is largely avoidable, positioning ES as a promising approach for continual learning in LLMs.

</details>


### [148] [RL2ML: Finite-Rollout Surrogate Objectives from Reinforcement Learning to Maximum Likelihood](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30154)
*Yifu Zheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 论文提出RL2ML，把RLVR里的有限采样训练目标系统化：它既能严格无偏地估计梯度，又能统一RL和ML风格目标。核心发现是，有限rollout下真正决定训练效果的是组级更新尺度、评测指标敏感性和方差，而不是目标看起来有多像最大似然。 / The paper introduces RL2ML, a unified framework for finite-rollout RLVR objectives with exactly unbiased gradients, bridging RL and ML-style training. The key takeaway is that performance under finite rollouts is governed by group-level update scaling, metric sensitivity, and variance, not simply by how close the objective is to maximum likelihood.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 现有RLVR中，人们常将期望层面优化的目标与有限rollout组带来的随机更新几何混为一谈，导致无法准确理解在固定采样预算下不同训练目标的真实行为与优劣。 / In RLVR, the expectation-level objective is often conflated with the stochastic update geometry induced by finite rollout groups, obscuring the true behavior and tradeoffs of training objectives under a fixed sampling budget.

Method: 提出RL2ML，一族有限rollout替代目标，具有闭式且严格无偏的梯度估计器；通过group-level update scale分析经验成功次数观测后组级重加权行为，并结合calibrated metric-gain analysis与exact variance decomposition研究目标选择。 / The paper proposes RL2ML, a family of finite-rollout surrogate objectives with a closed-form exactly unbiased gradient estimator; it analyzes group reweighting via the group-level update scale after observing empirical success counts, and combines calibrated metric-gain analysis with exact variance decomposition to study objective selection.

Result: RL2ML连续连接标准强化学习、类最大似然训练以及超越最大似然的目标；揭示了group-level update scale存在subcritical-supercritical transition，这一点在总体目标记号下不可见；并证明最佳替代目标取决于指标、敏感性与方差的联合关系。 / RL2ML continuously connects standard RL, maximum-likelihood-like training, and beyond-ML objectives; it reveals a subcritical-to-supercritical transition in group-level update scale hidden by population-level notation; and it shows that the best surrogate objective depends jointly on metric, sensitivity, and variance.

Conclusion: 在有限rollout预算下，RL2ML提供了一族与梯度估计器严格对齐、且梯度无偏的替代目标；最佳目标选择不应仅依据其是否接近最大似然或总体权重，而应由评测指标、局部敏感性和估计器方差共同决定，并可归约为一维优化问题。 / Under a fixed finite-rollout budget, RL2ML provides a family of surrogate objectives exactly aligned with an unbiased gradient estimator; the best objective is not determined by closeness to maximum likelihood or population-level weighting alone, but jointly by the evaluation metric, local sensitivity, and estimator variance, reducing selection to a one-dimensional optimization problem.

Abstract: Correctness-based Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) trains language models from binary feedback on sampled outputs, but the objective optimized in expectation and the stochastic update geometry induced by finite rollout groups are often conflated. This paper develops RL2ML, a family of finite-rollout surrogate objectives with a closed-form, exactly unbiased gradient estimator. The family continuously connects standard reinforcement learning, maximum-likelihood-like training, and beyond-maximum-likelihood objectives while preserving estimator-objective alignment under a fixed rollout budget. We introduce the group-level update scale to characterize how a rollout group is reweighted after its empirical success count is observed, revealing a subcritical-supercritical update-scale transition that is hidden by population-level objective notation alone. Building on this distinction, calibrated metric-gain analysis and exact variance decomposition show that the best choice of surrogate objective is determined neither by proximity to maximum likelihood nor by the population-level weight alone. Instead, it depends jointly on the evaluation metric, local sensitivity, and estimator variance. The remaining degree of freedom in the surrogate objective family can therefore be formulated as a one-dimensional optimization problem rather than treated as an unconstrained hyperparameter.

</details>


### [149] [On Distributional Reinforcement Learning in Chaotic Dynamical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30160)
*James Rudd-Jones,Mirco Musolesi,María Pérez-Ortiz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Chaotic dynamics make standard RL unstable because tiny state differences cause large trajectory divergence. This paper argues that optimizing return distributions, rather than only expected returns, produces a smoother and better-conditioned learning objective. Using the 1-Wasserstein metric, it shows why distributional RL is theoretically better matched to chaos.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In chaotic systems, exponential sensitivity to initial conditions makes bootstrap targets highly variable and gradient updates poorly conditioned. Since standard RL averages over diverging trajectories through scalar value functions, it mixes trajectory instability directly into the learning objective. The paper is motivated by the need for a more stable RL formulation in scientifically important chaotic domains.

Method: The paper provides a theoretical analysis of RL objectives in chaotic systems. It studies the evolution of return distributions under mild statistical stability assumptions and compares standard expected-return optimization with a distributional Bellman objective measured in the 1-Wasserstein metric.

Result: The main result is that, under mild assumptions, return distributions behave more regularly than trajectories themselves when evaluated with the 1-Wasserstein metric. This gives a smoother optimization landscape for distributional RL and explains why distributional methods can outperform standard RL in chaotic environments.

Conclusion: Distributional RL is better suited than standard scalar-value RL for chaotic dynamical systems because the return distribution evolves more smoothly than individual trajectories under the 1-Wasserstein metric, leading to a better-conditioned Bellman objective and more reliable learning.

Abstract: Chaotic dynamical systems pose a fundamental challenge for Reinforcement Learning (RL): exponential sensitivity to initial conditions induces high-variance bootstrap targets and poorly conditioned gradient updates. Chaotic dynamics arise across scientific and engineering domains, from fluid flows and climate systems to multi-agent systems, where reliable learning is highly desirable. Standard RL methods optimise expected returns through scalar value functions, implicitly averaging over diverging trajectories and entangling trajectory level instability with the learning objective. We show that under mild statistical stability assumptions, the return distribution evolves more regularly than individual trajectories when measured under the $1$-Wasserstein metric, yielding a smoother distributional Bellman objective. By aligning optimisation with this measure level structure, distributional RL provides better conditioned learning. We offer a principled explanation for the advantages of distributional methods in chaotic systems and the geometries of RL objectives under chaos.

</details>


### [150] [Can AI Weather Models Predict Beyond Two Weeks? A Quantitative Benchmark and Analysis of Long Rollouts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30184)
*Fanny Lehmann,Firat Ozdemir,Yun Cheng,Torsten Hoefler,Sebastian Schemm,Benedikt Soja,Siddhartha Mishra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper explains why AI weather models fail in long rollouts: long-term stability is mainly controlled by treatment of fine-scale noise. It classifies failures into blow-up, drift, and loss of seasonality, and shows that stable ViT-based models damp high-frequency perturbations instead of amplifying them.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although AI weather models perform well up to about 15 days, their long-term rollouts often become unstable, and the field lacks a formal framework to describe and diagnose these failures. The work aims to define such a taxonomy and identify the mechanisms behind stability.

Method: The paper performs year-long rollouts of nine state-of-the-art AI weather models, builds a taxonomy of long-horizon instability, analyzes spectral/high-frequency behavior and denoising response to noisy inputs, and validates the findings with ablation studies on Vision Transformer-based weather model architectures.

Result: The study identifies three instability regimes: blow-up, drift, and loss of seasonality. It finds that unstable models amplify high-frequency energy, whereas stable models behave like denoisers under input perturbations. Ablation studies further support that architectural design choices affecting small-scale processing are central to long-term stability.

Conclusion: AI weather model long-horizon stability depends strongly on how models handle small spatio-temporal scales. Stable models suppress high-frequency noise, generate distinct trajectories conditioned on the initial state, and avoid three identified failure regimes: blow-up, drift, and loss of seasonality.

Abstract: While AI weather models excel at short-to-medium range forecasts (up to 15 days), they frequently suffer from ill-defined "instabilities" when rolled out over longer horizons. This work addresses the lack of a formal taxonomy by categorizing these failures into three distinct regimes: blow-up, drift, and loss of seasonality, through year-long rollouts of nine state-of-the-art AI weather models. Our analysis reveals that stability hinges on the treatment of small spatio-temporal scales: unstable models amplify high-frequency energy, while stable models act as denoisers when noise is added to their inputs. Far from reducing these models to mere stochastic parrots, our findings highlight that stable models generate unique weather trajectories, conditioned on the initial state. We verify our findings through ablation studies on architectural design choices, conducted using state-of-the-art Vision Transformer (ViT) AI weather model architectures.

</details>


### [151] [HPO: Hysteretic Policy Optimization for Stable and Efficient Training under Sparse-Reward Regime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30201)
*Mohamed Sana,Nicola Piovesan,Antonio De Domenico,Fadhel Ayed,Haozhe Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper shows that GRPO can fail early in sparse-reward RL because negative-advantage samples dominate and length normalization distorts update size. A-HPO fixes this with adaptive down-weighting of negative updates plus mean-length normalization, yielding consistently better training efficiency and higher final rewards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GRPO-style RL suffers in sparse verifiable reward settings because early training often has more negatively advantaged responses than positively advantaged ones, causing harmful update imbalance. Response-level length normalization further couples update strength to response length, worsening optimization. The work aims to fix this narrow but common instability with a minimal algorithmic change.

Method: The paper identifies a GRPO failure mode where early batches contain more negative-advantage than positive-advantage responses, and per-response length normalization makes update magnitude depend on output length. It proposes HPO, which applies hysteresis by reducing the weight of negative-advantage updates and replaces response-level length normalization with mean-length normalization. It further proposes A-HPO, which adaptively sets the hysteretic weight from batch-level advantage-sign statistics.

Result: In TeleLogs and Countdown, A-HPO improves reward per update relative to GRPO, with the largest gains in early sparse-reward regimes. On TeleLogs, A-HPO reaches a final reward of 0.84, beating SAPO by 5%, GSPO by 11%, and GRPO by 15%, while keeping similar response length. On Countdown, it shows the strongest improvements in initial and hardest settings across 1.5B-7B models. Ablations indicate the gains come from better balancing positive and negative advantage contributions.

Conclusion: A-HPO is a simple, effective improvement over GRPO for sparse verifiable-reward RL. By down-weighting negative-advantage updates and using mean-length normalization, it improves reward per update, especially early in training, and outperforms GRPO, GSPO, and SAPO while keeping response length comparable.

Abstract: We investigate a narrow but common failure mode of GRPO-style reinforcement learning in the context of sparse verifiable rewards: early updates contain more responses with negative advantages than those with positive advantages, while response-level length normalization ties the magnitude of the update to the length of the output. We propose Hysteretic Policy Optimization (HPO), a minimal modification of GRPO that reduces the weight of negative-advantage updates and replaces per-response length normalization with mean-length normalization. We further introduce Adaptive HPO (A-HPO), which sets the hysteretic weight based on batch-level advantage-sign statistics, thereby removing the need for tuning a fixed hysteretic weight. In our TeleLogs and Countdown experiments, A-HPO improves the reward per update compared to GRPO, with the largest gains in early sparse reward regimes. On TeleLogs, A-HPO achieves a final reward of 0.84, outperforming SAPO by 5%, GSPO by 11%, and GRPO by 15%, while maintaining a comparable response-length. On Countdown, A-HPO achieves the largest gains in initial and most difficult configurations across 1.5B-7B models. Ablation studies on the hysteretic weight show that the gains of A-HPO come from better balancing the contributions of positive and negative advantages compared to positive-only or fully symmetric updates.

</details>


### [152] [How's it going? Reinforcement learning in language models recruits a functional welfare axis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30232)
*Andy Q Han,David J. Chalmers,Pavel Izmailov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RL appears to tap into an already-existing welfare-like internal direction in language models rather than inventing one; reward and punishment concept vectors generalize broadly and causally steer model behavior in opposite ways.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to understand how reinforcement learning changes a language model’s internal representations, especially whether reward optimization creates new internal structures or leverages existing ones. The paper focuses on whether there is a latent welfare-like representation that post-training can recruit.

Method: The authors train several language models in a semantically neutral maze environment using reinforcement learning, then extract concept vectors from rewarded and punished trajectories. They evaluate these vectors outside the maze setting by testing token preferences, alignment with emotion and goal-achievement concepts, and behavioral steering effects. They also run robustness controls across reward mappings, scale, instruct tuning, RL algorithms, model families, and fine-tuning methods, and compare RL with supervised fine-tuning.

Result: The punishment vector generalizes beyond the maze and behaves like a negative welfare representation: it increases failure/impossibility tendencies, aligns with negative emotion, tracks lower goal achievement, and induces refusal, uncertainty, backtracking, and negative self-reports when used for steering. The reward vector shows the opposite pattern, and both vectors are nearly antiparallel. These findings remain robust across many controls and are already visible before maze training, supporting the claim of a pre-existing welfare-like axis.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that reinforcement learning does not create a new internal notion of welfare in language models; instead, it recruits a pre-existing functional welfare axis that already exists before maze training and even in pretrain-only models. Minimal reward signals can therefore broadly shape behavior by activating this latent representation, which matters for interpretability, post-training dynamics, and alignment.

Abstract: How does reinforcement learning shape a language model's internal representations? We present evidence that RL recruits a pre-existing representation of functional welfare: an estimate of how well or badly the system is doing, relative to its goals. We train several language models in a novel, semantically neutral maze environment. We then extract concept vectors for rewarded and punished trajectories, and evaluate those vectors in settings unrelated to the maze environment. The punishment vector behaves like a representation of negative welfare: it promotes failure and impossibility tokens, it aligns with negative emotion concepts, it negatively tracks goal-achievement, and steering with it induces negative self-reports, pathological backtracking, refusal, and uncertainty. The positive reward vector behaves as the mirror image, and the two are nearly antiparallel. These effects are robust when controlling for tile-to-reward mapping, scale, instruct tuning, RL training algorithm, model family, and LoRA versus full-finetuning, and largely persist when we replace RL with supervised fine-tuning. Importantly, the vectors are effective in models before they have undergone maze training. Combined with observations that the effects also appear in pretrain-only models, we therefore argue that this functional welfare axis pre-exists post-training: it is recruited, rather than created, by post-training. While we make no claims about any experience of welfare, the axis offers a demonstration that minimal reward signals can broadly affect model behavior by recruiting pre-existing welfare-like representations, with implications for interpretability, post-training dynamics, and alignment.

</details>


### [153] [OOD-GraphLLM: Graph Large Language Model for Out-of-Distribution Generalized Drug Synergy Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30247)
*Xin Wang,Linxin Xiao,Yang Yao,Wenwu Zhu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OOD-GraphLLM is a graph+LLM framework for drug synergy prediction that targets out-of-distribution molecular shifts by jointly learning graph structure and biomedical semantics, using finetuned DrugSyn-LLM plus retrieval-augmented instruction tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Drug synergy prediction faces out-of-distribution shifts because new compounds bring different molecular scaffolds and sizes, breaking the in-distribution assumption used by prior methods. The goal is to generalize to unseen molecular structures while capturing both structural and semantic biomedical information.

Method: The authors propose OOD-GraphLLM, which combines graph neural network-based molecular representation learning with a biomedical large language model. They finetune DrugSyn-LLM and use retrieval-augmented biomedical instruction tuning to align molecular topological information and semantic information for language-based reasoning under O.O.D. settings.

Result: The paper reports a novel graphLLM framework for O.O.D. generalized drug synergy prediction and claims it can accurately predict drug synergy under O.O.D. settings. It also releases source code, a model, and a web interface for public use.

Conclusion: This paper concludes that OOD-GraphLLM can improve out-of-distribution generalized drug synergy prediction by jointly modeling molecular graph structure and biomedical language semantics in a unified LLM-based framework.

Abstract: Drug synergy prediction (DSP) aims to identify efficacious drug combinations under various cellular contexts with different targets. However, the continual emergence of novel compounds results in variations in molecular scaffolds and sizes, causing drug synergy data to exhibit out-of-distribution (O.O.D.) shifts with respect to topological structure. Existing works rely on in-distribution (I.D.) assumption, failing to handle the O.O.D. shifts. To solve this problem, we study out-of-distribution generalized drug synergy prediction through a graph large language model for the first time. Nevertheless, O.O.D. generalized DSP is highly non-trivial, posing several challenges: i) how to discover structurally relevant and irrelevant molecular representations with respect to cell targets; ii) how to find the optimal graph neural architectures that accurately calculate molecular representations; and iii) how to jointly leverage molecular structural and semantic information in LLMs. To address these challenges, we propose OOD-GraphLLM, a novel graphLLM framework which is able to accurately predict drug synergy under O.O.D. settings via jointly optimizing molecular graph representation and biomedical semantic language representations in a unified manner. Furthermore, we finetune DrugSyn-LLM, a biomedical LLM, and employ a retrieval-augmented biomedical instruction tuning strategy to align molecular topological information and molecular semantic information with language-based reasoning for O.O.D. generalized DSP. Both the source code (https://github.com/EkkoXiao/Bio-GraphLLM) and released model (https://mn.cs.tsinghua.edu.cn/bio-graphllm/) are publicly available, where users are allowed to download model resources and interactively use the system through a web interface.

</details>


<div id='cs.CE'></div>

# cs.CE [[Back]](#toc)

### [154] [Comparative Analysis of Compliance-Matrix Induced Norms in Structural Topology Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28857)
*Jyotiranjan Nayak,Shafeequdheen P,Vijayakrishna Rowthu*

Main category: cs.CE

TL;DR: 这篇论文说明：柔顺度怎么定义，不只是数学重写，而会真正改变拓扑优化结果。经典目标得到更均匀的受力路径，l1型目标得到更稀疏、更集中的结构。 The paper shows that how compliance is defined is not just a mathematical rewrite; it materially changes topology optimization outcomes. The classical objective gives more uniform load paths, while the l1-type objective produces sparser, more concentrated structures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 传统上柔顺度最小化通常默认采用经典二次形式，但这一选择未必是唯一合理的。作者希望检验：当柔顺度用不同范数形式表达时，是否会改变优化景观与生成的结构形态，从而为定制结构性能提供更多设计自由。 Classical compliance minimization usually defaults to the quadratic form, but that choice is not necessarily unique or neutral. The authors aim to test whether alternative norm-based compliance formulations change the optimization landscape and resulting structural morphology, enabling more tailored structural performance.

Method: 论文比较了三种基于结构能量范数表示的拓扑优化目标：经典二次柔顺度、其平方根形式（对应l2范数）、以及由刚度加权位移场构造的谱l1范数形式，并通过数值实验分析它们对优化结果的影响。 The paper compares three topology optimization objectives based on norm representations of structural energy: classical quadratic compliance, its square-root form (an l2 norm), and a spectral l1-norm formulation derived from the stiffness-weighted displacement field, then evaluates their effects through numerical experiments.

Result: 数值结果表明，经典二次柔顺度倾向于形成分布均衡的载荷传递路径；基于l1的谱范数目标则更倾向于生成稀疏、局部化更强的结构构件。不同表述即使物理来源一致，也会带来明显不同的拓扑结果。 Numerical results show that classical quadratic compliance yields well-distributed load paths, whereas the spectral l1-based objective promotes sparse, highly localized members. Even with the same physical origin, the formulations produce clearly different topologies.

Conclusion: 不同的柔顺度目标虽然基于相同的刚度-位移关系，但会诱导出显著不同的优化地形与最终拓扑；目标函数的选择会直接决定载荷路径分布、构件稀疏性与局部化程度。 Different compliance objectives derived from the same stiffness-displacement relation create substantially different optimization landscapes and topologies; objective selection directly controls load-path distribution, sparsity, and localization of the resulting structure.

Abstract: Compliance minimization is a central objective in structural topology optimization, commonly interpreted as the total strain energy of a system. In this work, we examine the influence of alternative compliance formulations based on different norm representations of structural energy. Specifically, we consider three formulations: the classical quadratic compliance, its square-root form corresponding to an l2 norm, and a spectral l1 -norm based formulation derived from the stiffness weighted displacement field. Although these formulations arise from the same stiffness displacement relationship, they generate markedly different optimization landscapes and result in distinct structural topologies. Numerical results indicate that the classical formulation produces well-distributed load paths, whereas the l1 -based formulation promotes sparse and highly localized structural members. These findings underscore the critical role of objective function selection in topology optimization and offer insights into alternative formulations for achieving tailored structural performance.

</details>


### [155] [Automated design of soft-rigid hybrid robots for dynamic locomotion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29389)
*Hiroki Kobayashi,Yuki Takaha,Changyoung Yuhn,Yuki Sato,Sunao Tomita,Atsushi Kawamoto,Tsuyoshi Nomura*

Main category: cs.CE

TL;DR: An automated differentiable design method creates soft-rigid hybrid robots by co-optimizing soft shape, rigid truss skeleton, and actuation; the fabricated robot successfully walks as predicted, and the internal skeleton is key to producing useful resonance-like deformation for locomotion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to bridge the gap between rigid robots, which are strong but insufficiently compliant for unstructured environments, and fully soft robots, which are adaptable but limited in scalability and load-bearing capacity, by drawing inspiration from biological musculoskeletal systems that combine hard and soft tissues.

Method: The authors propose an automated, gradient-based design pipeline for soft-rigid hybrid robots that jointly optimizes freeform soft-body geometry, stiff truss skeleton layout, and multi-channel actuation using a differentiable simulator coupling MPM for soft materials with XPBD for truss elements.

Result: The optimized robot was fabricated and tested on a walking task. Experiments matched the predicted walking behavior, showed that the gait does not emerge without the skeleton, and modal analysis indicated that the skeleton creates deformation modes near the driving frequency that support effective stride generation.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that an optimized soft-rigid hybrid with an internal truss skeleton can enable locomotion patterns that a purely soft body does not exhibit, and that the skeleton likely improves walking by introducing deformation modes near the actuation frequency.

Abstract: Rigid-bodied robots often lack compliance needed to adapt to unstructured environments, while fully soft robots, though highly adaptable, struggle with scalability and load capacity. In nature, musculoskeletal systems balance strength and flexibility by integrating hard and soft tissues. Inspired by this principle, we present an automated design method for soft-rigid hybrids that optimizes a freeform soft-body shape, a stiff truss layout, and multi-channel actuation. Our differentiable simulator couples the material point method (MPM) for deformable bodies with extended position-based dynamics (XPBD) for truss elements, enabling gradient-based search. The optimization generates truss skeletons that transmit actuation forces to the soft body. We fabricate the optimized design and evaluate it on a walking task. Experiments reproduce the walking mode predicted by the optimization, which does not emerge without the skeleton. Modal analysis further suggests that the skeleton enables deformation modes near the actuation frequency that promote effective stride generation.

</details>


### [156] [A Lumped RC Equivalent Circuit Model of Head Tissues in sub-MHz Frequency Regimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29996)
*Angelo Faccia,Ermanno Citraro,Francesco P. Andriulli*

Main category: cs.CE

TL;DR: A lightweight RC head model reproduces sub-megahertz electrical behavior of a layered spherical head with close validation against analytical references, making it useful for fast simulation and design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate, fast modeling of electric potential and current distribution in head tissues is needed for neuro-sensing and neuro-stimulation design, especially where full numerical electromagnetic simulation is too costly for prototyping, real-time use, and circuit-level integration.

Method: The paper builds a lumped RC equivalent circuit for a canonical three-layer spherical head geometry, incorporating frequency-dependent tissue conductivity and permittivity via complex conductivity in the electro-quasi-static regime, then validates it against a dipolar brain source configuration.

Result: The proposed model matches semi-analytical solutions closely across skull thicknesses and dipole eccentricities, and the study quantifies how tissue dispersion and displacement current improve accuracy.

Conclusion: A minimal lumped RC circuit can approximate the electrical behavior of a three-layer spherical head model up to 50 kHz, with good agreement to semi-analytical validation for scalp voltage peak estimation.

Abstract: Accurate modeling of electric potential and current distribution in head tissues is crucial for the design and evaluation of neuro-sensing and neuro-stimulation systems operating in the sub megahertz frequency range. Numerical methods are widely employed in electromagnetic simulations, however their computational cost can limit their applicability to rapid prototyping, real-time simulations, and circuit-level integration. In this work, we introduce a lumped RC equivalent circuit model that reproduces the electrical behavior of a canonical three-layer spherical head geometry over a frequency range up to 50 kHz. The model accounts for frequency-dependent tissue conductivity and permittivity to capture dispersive effects, employing complex conductivity in the electro-quasi-static (EQS) regime. The circuit topology uses a minimal set of impedance elements in order to represent the essential mechanisms of electric signal propagation. Validation was performed using a dipolar brain source configuration for scalp voltage peak estimation, showing close agreement with semi-analytical solutions across different skull thicknesses and dipole eccentricities. In addition, the impact of tissue dispersion and displacement current inclusion on the model accuracy was quantitatively assessed, highlighting their contribution to the overall fidelity of the proposed approach.

</details>


### [157] [A Lumped-Element Electrical Model of the Human Head for Brain-Oriented Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30172)
*Angelo Faccia,Ermanno Citraro,Francesco P. Andriulli*

Main category: cs.CE

TL;DR: The paper builds a small RC-circuit substitute for the brain-skull-scalp system that preserves frequency-dependent electrical behavior and matches reference solutions well; simplified non-dispersive models are less accurate and tend to overpredict scalp voltage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full EQS head models can be complex and computationally expensive. The work is motivated by the need for a compact circuit-level surrogate that still captures anisotropic pathways and frequency-dispersive tissue behavior accurately.

Method: The paper models a three-shell head geometry (brain, skull, scalp) using a surrogate circuit with separate radial and tangential RC branches for each layer. Frequency-dependent conductivity and permittivity are translated into dispersive resistive and capacitive circuit elements, and the model is validated against a semi-analytical spherical-harmonics reference solution over multiple geometries and frequencies.

Result: The surrogate circuit shows good agreement with the semi-analytical reference across different configurations and frequencies. Models that ignore dispersion or capacitive branches overestimate scalp potentials within the studied frequency range.

Conclusion: A compact dispersive RC surrogate circuit can accurately model electro-quasi-static head behavior across geometries and frequencies, and including both dispersion and capacitive pathways is necessary to avoid overestimating scalp potentials.

Abstract: In this work, we present a compact surrogate circuit for electro-quasi-static (EQS) head modeling. A three-shell geometry (brain, skull, scalp) is considered, and each layer is modeled through radial and tangential pathways, implemented as RC branches. Frequency-dependent tissue conductivity and permittivity are mapped into dispersive resistive and capacitive elements. The model is validated against a semi-analytical spherical-harmonics reference solution over multiple geometrical configurations and operating frequencies, demonstrating good agreement. Neglecting dispersion and capacitive pathways can lead to an overestimation of scalp potentials over the considered frequency range, highlighting the need for dispersive RC circuit modeling.

</details>
